Dynamics, data and reconstruction
Suddhasattwa Das, Tomoharu Suda

Abstract
The goal of data-driven learning of dynamical systems is to interpret time series as a continuous observation of an underlying dynamical system. This task is not well-posed for a variety of reasons - such as multiple co-existing sub-systems, topologically inter-weaving of these sub-systems; and more importantly, the non-injectivity of the correspondence between dynamical systems and time series. We show how these ambiguities are circumvented if one considers dynamical systems and measurement maps collectively. Dynamical systems, observed dynamical systems, and time series data - each of these three collections have an extensive network of relations within them, which gives them the mathematical structure of a category. One of the new concepts proposed is a rigorous definition of time series data as a chain of measurement sequences with decreasing information content. This definition subsumes the familiar notions of sequences, time series and even subshifts. Using these notions it is shown that the entire process of converting an observed dynamical systems into a time series object is functorial, and passes through a number of phases each bearing its own categorical structure. This discovery sheds new light on the nature of reconstruction algorithms. Under mild conditions of consistency, reconstruction itself is shown to be functorial operation. This provides a new category theoretic perspective on the nature and limits of reconstruction.



Key words.
Semi-conjugacy, reconstruction, semi-group, functor, Kan-extension, adjoints, time series

AMS subject classifications.
18D25, 18A40, 37M99, 18F60, 37M22, 18A32, 18A25, 37M10

1  Introduction

The mathematical notion of a dynamical system - a space
\Omega
along with group of transformations
\Phi^{t}:\Omega\to\Omega
is present in most natural, mechanical and human-driven systems, such as the climate, ecological models, controls systems, and the stock market. The points in the space
\Omega
represent the states the system could be in. The index
t
represents time and can be either a continuous or discrete variable. For each time
t
,
\Phi^{t}
represents a transformation or rearrangement of the states. This leads to different kinds of phenomena - statistical, topological and geometric.

However the pair
(\Omega,\Phi^{t})
itself may not be visible. For example in climate, epidemiological phenomena, the phase space
\Omega
is not known or too abstract. Sometimes
\Omega
may be known but the dynamical law
\Phi^{t}
is not apparent. The dynamical system is usually indirectly observed via an observation or measurement map
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then for any initial point
\omega
of
\Omega
, and an orbit
\left\{\Phi^{t}\omega\;:\;0\leq t\leq T\right\}
up to time
T
, one obtains a time series
\left\{\phi\left(\Phi^{t}\omega\right)\;:\;0\leq t\leq T\right\}
of
d
-dimensional data points. The task of
data-driven discovery
of dynamical systems is to attempt a reconstruction of the underlying dynamical system solely from such time series
(
1
,
2
, e.g.)
.

Our main result is based on a mathematical formulation of the above notions. We state later in Theorem
7
that an observation-based inner approximation is also observation-exact. This statement requires a precise understanding of the notions of “observation”, ”reconstruction”, ”data-driven”, ”inner-approximation” and ”exactness”. These will be gradually explained throughout the paper. The approach to be presented in this paper works not only for space valued dynamics and observations, where the notion of quantitative approximation can be used, but also for a fairly broad types of settings such as measurable spaces or pre-ordered sets, which would be beyond the scope of most reconstruction algorithms. For example, we can observe a measurable dynamics via a measurable partition of the phase space. This will result in a discrete valued data not immediately amenable to quantitative approximations such as least squares methods. However, our result indicates how to approximate dynamics from such qualitative data and what guess will be the most reasonable.

1.
Choose a network with countable number of nodes, each node representing an
observed dynamical system
. Nodes are graded to indicate levels of complexity.

2.
For any data set
X
and complexity level
L
use the network to perform an inner or outer approximation for
X
.

3.
Devise an algorithm that implements this approximation scheme under various constraints such as size of hypothesis space and precision of regression algorithms.

Figure 1
:
General scheme for devising algorithms for functorial reconstruction of dynamics.

The task of reconstruction involves the following different mathematical notions - dynamical systems, measurement, orbits, time series, data and reconstructed model itself. All these terms are related but different. To assess how effective a reconstruction algorithm is, it is important to separate the notions of a dynamical system, a dataset, and an algorithm. The separation of these concepts lead to the following questions :

1.

An algorithm takes as input a dataset and outputs a dynamical system. The output is created regardless of whether the dataset arises from a dynamical system. In the general case, how is the output related to the dataset?

2.

An algorithm should be consistent. This means that with more information, i.e., a larger dataset, the estimation should get better. What should be the mathematical definition of consistency ?

3.

Different dynamical systems may yield the same time series, depending on the measurement. How does this non-uniqueness affect the convergence of reconstructed dynamical systems ?

Question 1 (
abbrev.
Q1) addresses the difference between a time series generated by a true orbit, and the general notion of dataset. As has been discovered in recent works, the outcome of numerical methods is strongly dictated by an aspect of dynamical systems called its
spectrum
or
spectral measure

(
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
, e.g.)
. Most numerical procedures involve some form of averaging, whose limiting value reveals only selected aspects of the dynamics. This reveals one of the many complications associated to Q1.

Q2 addresses the issue of consistency which is relevant in most branches of approximation theory. One would expect that if a sequence of datasets has increasing information content, an algorithm should be able to utilize that to provide progressively better approximations. Algorithms have been defined in the context of computability and symbolic dynamics
(
7
,
8
, e.g.)
but a general definition is hard to provide. Our answer to Q2 is via a category called
time series data
, an approach quite different from other characterizations of information flow
(
9
,
10
, e.g.)
.

Dynamical systems tend to have a
nested structure
of sub-systems embedded within sub-systems
[
13
]
. One often relies on the assumption of
ergodicity
to set a definite limit for empirical measures and time series. However, even ergodic dynamical systems which are chaotic are known to have a dense collection of periodic orbits
[
14
,
15
]
. Thus these periodic orbits mimic the invariant set and measure of the true dynamics. Moreover, they have been shown to mimic many other ergodic and asymptotic aspects of the dynamics, such as entropy, pressure and dimensions
[
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
. However, periodic orbits have properties different from the general dynamics - their Lyapunov exponents are zero, and they are non-mixing. These results indicate the difficulty of addressing the issues raised in Q3.

We present four new ideas and perspectives in the search for answers to these questions. The first is that the entire process of data generation from measurements of a dynamical system is a
functorial
process, presented in the diagram below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{\text{DSM}}\\
\mbox{Dynamics}\\
+\mbox{Measurement}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{\text{DSMO}}\\
\mbox{Dynamics}\\
+\mbox{Measurement}\\
+\mbox{Orbits}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{\text{DSMO}}_{\mathbb{N}}\\
\mbox{Discrete-time}\\
\mbox{dynamics}\\
+\mbox{Measurement}\\
+\mbox{Orbits}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{\text{DS}}\\
\mbox{Dynamical}\\
\mbox{systems}\\
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Subshifts}\\
\Seq\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{\text{DS}}\\
\mbox{Dynamical}\\
\mbox{systems}\\
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\TSD\\
\mbox{Timeseries}\\
\mbox{data}\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Dscrt}_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\Recon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Data_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Recon}

(1)

The figure above helps pose Questions 1–3 above in a precise mathematical setting, by presenting the various entities discussed. The precise role and mathematical nature of reconstruction may be understood only if the process of generation of data is modeled correctly. Diagram
1
presents data generation via a correspondence
\Data
between two collections labeled
DSMO
and
\TSD
respectively. Each object in
DSMO
is a combination of a dynamical system, a measurement, and orbits in the phase space. Each object in
\TSD
is a collections of chunks of data with increasing information content. The correspondence
\Data
brings to light that the source of data is not a dynamical system alone, but a more complicated
DSMO
object. Thus two different dynamical systems may map into different
DSMO
objects, which generate the exact same time series via the transformation labeled as
\Data
. Although dynamics is our primary object of interest, it only forms one of the components of
DSMO
.

The correspondence
\Data
is not just a mapping between objects. We will see later in Section
3
that one can define
morphisms
or relationships between the compound objects in
DSMO
, which represent a combined conjugacy between dynamics, orbits and observations. These relations are compositional, and lead to a structure called a
category
. Further, we shall see that a morphism
\phi:X\to X^{\prime}
between
DSMO
-objects is reflected as a hierarchy of information content in their generated time series in
\TSD
. Thus
\Data
is a correspondence that also preserves the structure present in these categories, making it a
functor
.
In fact each node in Diagram
1
is a category of its own, and each arrow a functor. So Diagram
1
is not just a flowchart but the layout of how during the process of measuring and reconstructing a dynamical system, various structure preserving transformations take place. The functorial property of the maps in the diagram are not imposed or assumed, they are derived as consequences. We formulate these notions for a fairly general class of categories.

Our second main idea is a mathematical notion of a
time series data
object
\TSD
, made precise using category theoretic language in Section
8
. A
\TSD
object is a concept independent of the concepts of dynamical system and number sequences. It models a sequence of growing information content that is fed to an algorithm. This is done with a generality offered by our Assumptions
1
and
2
which allows
\TSD
objects to take the form of not just numerical sequences but also preorders and partitions.

The third important point we make in Sections
9
and
10
is that under mild assumptions, the limiting or idealized behavior of reconstruction algorithms are themselves functorial. So while an algorithm may not be structure preserving, its underlying scheme, representing its limiting behavior, is functorial. The arrow
\Recon
in Diagram
1
represents a general data-driven reconstruction scheme. The confluence of two functors –
\Data
and
\Recon
provides a host of category theoretic tools to analyze them.

Our fourth and final point is the perspective that the design of reconstruction algorithms and judgment of their efficacy should not be based on individual cases, as argued above in Q3. While the results of an algorithm may be compared and evaluated for individual cases, the correctness of an algorithmic
scheme
is hard to be defined. We use our category theoretic framework in Section
10
to promote the idea that a reconstruction scheme should ultimately be an inner or outer approximation. An inner or outer approximation requires a collection of reference objects along with a hierarchical structure. This is presented as the second stage of Figure
1
. This reference structure and approximation strategy are made rigorous in Definition
24
and Theorem
9
. Note that the choice of reference objects also a part of the design and presented as the first stage. The actual algorithm design is an implementation, often imprecise, of this approximation scheme. This is presented as the third and final stage of Figure
1
.

Thus, although any reconstruction algorithm may be built without regard for functoriality, their efficacy or correction can only be assessed in a categorical framework. This requires placing the algorithm in a proper context, as suggested in Figure
1
.

This work does not prescribe any numerical recipe or technique, but aims to serve as a general framework to describe and analyze such methods in general settings. For example, our analysis indicates that there is no universal data-driven reconstruction technique that provides an exact reconstruction. This is expressed as Conjecture
1
in Section
10
. Regardless of which computational tools such as neural networks, kernel-methods, or DMD are used, algorithms should aim to capture the collective categorical structure present within dynamical systems.

Outline

We begin with a brief review of category theory in Section
2
. We next realize the confluence of dynamics, measurements and orbits as a category, using the language of comma categories in Section
3
. Next Section
4
presents the combined notion of dynamical systems being observed along orbits as a mathematical object. Section
5
presents some consequences of our assumptions made in Section
2
. This results in the creation of a category theoretic notion of subshifts in Section
6
. Section
7
next explains how subshifts arise naturally from measurements of dynamical systems. Next Section
8
introduces time series data as a rigorous mathematical object amenable for categorical analysis. Section
9
discusses the functorial nature of reconstruction, and Section
10
discusses various properties of reconstruction as a functor. Some technical lemmas and proofs have been moved to the Appendix.

2  A categorical perspective for dynamics

The usual approach to dynamical systems is analytical – one assumes some properties on a space and a dynamics law, and studies the consequences of those properties. The structure provides the context for the study. In a set-theoretic context one looks for features such as invariant sets, fixed points, and periodic orbits. In a topological context one looks for features such as attractors and stable and unstable manifolds. In a measure theoretic context, features such as invariant measures and ergodic measures. A categorical viewpoint does not make separate considerations for these contexts. All the definitions assume a generic context and only takes into account the network of relations that exist within the context. Spaces and maps lose all of their internal content and exist as symbolic points as arrows. The objective is to re-establish all the various properties as consequences of these network of relations. We begin with a brief introduction to the notion of categories and functors.

Category

A category
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of two kinds of entities :

1.

objects : usually representing different instances of the same mathematical construct; We will often write
x\in\mathcal{C}
to mean that
x
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
.

2.

morphism : connecting arrows between a pair of objects; satisfying the following three properties –

3.

compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
, the morphisms can be joined end-to-end to create a composite morphism represented as
a\xrightarrow{g\circ f}b
;

4.

associativity : the composition of morphisms is associative;

5.

identity morphism : each object
a
is endowed with a morphism
\Id_{x}
called the
identity
morphism, which play the role of unit element in composition.

Given two objects
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}
, the collection of arrows from
x
to
y
is denoted as
\Hom(x;y)
. Note that this collection may be infinite, finite or even empty. The last criterion implies that for each
x

\Hom(x;x)
has at least one member. Whenever there are multiple categories being discussed, one uses the notations
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)
or
\mathcal{C}(x;y)
to indicate that the morphisms are within the category
\mathcal{C}
.

Examples

One of the most fundamental categories is
Set
, the category in which objects are sets up to a certain prefixed cardinality, and arrows are arbitrary maps. Similarly
Topo
denotes the category of topological spaces, with continuous maps as arrows. We denote by
Vec
the category in which the objects are vector spaces and arrows are linear maps. The collection
Affine
that we have already defined has the same objects as
Vec
but all affine maps as morphisms. Note that this includes the morphisms in
Vec
. This makes
Vec
a
subcategory
of
Affine
. Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is any set. Then the power-set
2^{\mathcal{U}}
of subsets of
\mathcal{U}
is a category, in which the relations are the subset
\subseteq
relations. Note that there can be only at most one arrow between any two objects
A,B
of this category, which is to be interpreted as inclusion. Such categories are known as
preorders
, and other examples are the category of ordered natural numbers, real numbers, open covers, and the concept of infinitesimal
(
20
, see)
.

A particularly important example of categorical structure can be found in semigroups. Any semigroup
\mathcal{G}
can be interpreted as a 1-point category, in which each arrow corresponds bijectively to elements of
\mathcal{G}
. The associativity of semigroup operation becomes the associativity of arrow composition. Figure
2
presents some 1-point categorical representations of the semigroups
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
,
\left[\mathbb{Z},+\right]
and
\left[\mathbb{R},+\right]
. See Figure
2
for some illustrations. We next discuss how different categories can be transformed into one another.

Figure 2
:
Representing semi-groups as 1-object categories –
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
,
\left[\mathbb{Z},+\right]
and
\left[\mathbb{R},+\right]
.

Functors

Given two categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
, a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a mapping between their objects along with the following properties :

1.

For each
x,y\in ob(\mathcal{C})
,
F
induces a map
F_{x,y}:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)\to\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(Fx;Fy)
. Thus arrows / morphisms between any pair of points get mapped into morphisms between the corresponding pair of points in the image.

2.

F
preserves compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
,
F(g\circ f)=F(g)\circ F(f)
.

3.

F
preserves identity :
F(\Id_{a})=\Id_{F(a)}
.

Functors are thus maps that also preserve categorical structure, mainly by preserving compositionality. One of the simplest notions of functors are monotonic functions from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
, and may be interpreted as functors from
\left[\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right]
, which is the category defined by
\mathbb{R}
with the preorder
\leq
, to
\left[\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right]
or
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
, which is the category defined by
\mathbb{R}
with
\geq
. Realizing mathematical transformations as functors leads to deeper insights in the associated field. Some examples are Lebesgue integration
[
21
]
, diameter and Lebesgue number
[
20
]
, and topological closure
[
22
]
.

One can interpret a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
as drawing of the
\mathcal{C}
within
\mathcal{D}
. The objects and arrows in
\mathcal{C}
are overlaid on objects and arrows in
\mathcal{D}
. For that reason a functor is also called a
diagram
or
\mathcal{C}
-diagram if its domain is
\mathcal{C}
. There is a structure preserving manner in which
\mathcal{C}
-diagrams may be transformed. This leads us to the next topic :

Natural transformations

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be two categories, and
F,F^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be two functors. A natural transformation
\alpha
from
F
to
F^{\prime}
, denoted as
\alpha:F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
is a family of arrows
\left\{\alpha_{c}:F(c)\to F^{\prime}(c)\;:\;c\in ob(\mathcal{C})\right\}
such that the following commutations hold

(2)

Just like arrows / morphisms connect objects, natural transformations connect functors. In fact the collection
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{C}\;;\;\mathcal{D}\right)
of functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
is itself a category, in which the morphisms are natural transformations. This realization that the collection of functors itself is a category bears a lot of significance in the study of homology, simplicial complexes and sheaf theory in general. We will find a special significance in the study of dynamical systems.

Category of dynamical systems

Any dynamical system has a concept of time associated to it. The role of time can be played by any semigroup, such as
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)
,
(\mathbb{N},+)
,
(\mathbb{R}_{0},+)
and
(\mathbb{R},+)
. Such a time semigroup shall be denoted by
\mathcal{T}
. We shall interpret it as a 1-point category. Next let us fix a category
\Context
and call it the
context
category. It is usually taken to be
Topo
,
Vec
or
Meas
, the category of measurable spaces and measurable maps. The category
\Context
represents the nature of spaces and maps that are involved our dynamical systems of interest. The context and time are the two ingredients of a dynamical systems.

The collection of dynamical systems evolving according to time interpretation
\mathcal{T}
and within the context
\Context
, forms the functor category

\textbf{\text{DS}}_{\Context}^{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right).

If the categories
\mathcal{T}
and
\Context
are clear from context, we shall denote
\textbf{\text{DS}}_{\Context}^{\mathcal{T}}
simply as
DS
. Note that each object in this category is a functor
\Phi:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
. This involves selecting an object
\Omega
from
\Context
and a family of endomorphisms
\Phi^{t}:\Omega\to\Omega
indexed by
\left\{t\in\mathcal{T}\right\}
. This is exactly the definition of a dynamical system, or more precisely, an action of the semigroup
\mathcal{T}
on
\Omega
. The diagram below shows the case when
\mathcal{T}=\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
. See Figure
3
for an illustration.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

Figure 3
:
Dynamics as a functor. Section
2
presents a dynamical system as a functor between a semigroup category
\mathcal{T}
representing time, and a category
\mathcal{C}
representing the context. Shown here is such a functor
\Phi
transforming the additive semigroup
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
of non-negative integers into self-maps on a space
\Omega
. Since
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
is generated by the unit element
1
, the image
F
of this element generates all iterations of the dynamics.

This interpretation of dynamical systems as a functor category
DS
has provided new insights into the study of dynamical systems and their asymptotic properties
(
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
, e.g.)
. The morphisms in
DS
also have a very useful interpretation. Given two objects
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\in\textbf{\text{DS}}
, a morphism between them in
DS
is a natural transformation
\alpha:\Phi\Rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}
. This means that the following commutations hold

(3)

as dictated by (
2
). This commutation turns out to be exactly the concept of
semi-conjugacy
in Dynamical systems theory. Since
\mathcal{T}
is a 1-object category, the natural transformation
\alpha
has only 1 connecting morphism
\alpha_{\Omega}
. Thus without ambiguity and by reuse of notation, we denote the one and only connecting morphism of the natural transform
\alpha
also as
\alpha
. If
\alpha
is surjective, then the dynamics
\Phi^{\prime t}
can be considered to be a partial measurement of the system which evolves autonomously. The following commuting diagram illustrates the case when
\mathcal{T}=\mathbb{N}_{0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}^{\prime}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{2}}

One dynamical system may be contained in another, or may be a factor of the other. Both relations are special instances of the morphism (
3
). Thus recognizing the categorical structure of the collection of all time-
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{C}
-dynamical systems also implicitly takes into accounts all these relations. At this point we make a technical assumption on the categorical nature of the context
\Context
:

Assumption 1
.

The context category
\Context
is a topological concrete category, with
U:\Context\to\textbf{\text{Set}}
being its forgetful functor.

The notion of a topological concrete category
(
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
, e.g.)
extracts certain categorical / structural properties of
Topo
to enable a more general and abstract category in which several familiar notions of topology can be realized along with their usual properties. Thus while
Topo
remains an example of Assumption
1
, other categories such as
Meas
are also examples. Throughout our discussion, several properties of such a category
\Context
will be invoked, each capturing different aspects of topological space. Section
11.4
presents some technical details.

Time series is not directly generated from a dynamical system, but from measurements made along trajectories of the dynamics. To capture the data-generation process as a functor, we need to establish a category which combines dynamics as well as measurements.

3  Observations

Table 1
:
Instances of Assumption
2
. The following instances of
\Context_{obs}
,
\Context
and
\iota
are commonly encountered in data-driven approaches to dynamical system reconstruction.

\Context

\Context_{obs}

Interpretation of
\iota:\Context_{obs}\to\Context

Topo

\FinSet

Observing continuous dynamics on topological spaces via finite-state machines

Topo

Vec

Real-valued measurements of continuous dynamics on topological spaces, along with linear transforms of the data.

Meas

\FinSet

Measurable dynamics being observed by finite measurable partitions

Topo

Alex

Morse-decomposition calculations of continuous dynamics on topological spaces, along with order preserving transformations of the data. See Example
1

The simplest interpretation of a dynamical system along with a measurement, is a dynamical system
\Phi^{t}:\Omega\to\Omega
along with a map
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. Instead of considering only real-valued measurements, we shall allow more general measurement spaces
\mathcal{Y}
drawn from some category
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
. To prescribe our assumptions on
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
we need to recall certain categorical notions. A morphism
f:a\to b
is said to be
monic
/
injective
if for any third object
c
and two morphisms
g,g^{\prime}:c\to a
, the equality
fg=fg^{\prime}
holds iff
g=g^{\prime}
. Note that in the context of
Set
this definition is equivalent to the set-theoretic notion of injectivity. One can similarly define a morphism to be
epic
/
surjective
. Given an object
b
, a
subobject
of
b
refers to any injection
f:a\to b
. Injections and surjections will be depicted by hooked
and double-headed
arrows respectively. One of the most important properties of being topological concrete is as follows :

Property 1
.

A category
\Context
satisfying Assumption
1
has products, finite and infinite.

In
Set
,
Meas
and
Topo
, products are simply cartesian products. A product of an indexed family of spaces
\left\{a_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
is denoted as
a:=\prod_{i\in I}a_{i}
. It is equipped with coordinate-wise projection maps
\pi_{i}:a\to a_{i}
. These projection maps are also
universal
in the sense that any collection of functions
f_{i}:b\to a_{i}
from a common domain
b
must factor through a unique map
\phi:b\to a
. The categorical notion of products is a structural generalization of these ideas. Section
11.1
presents more details.

Assumption 2
.

There is an observation category
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
, and a functor
\iota:\mathcal{C}_{obs}\to\Context
s.t.

1.

\mathcal{C}_{obs}
has finite direct products and
\iota
preserves them.

2.

\mathcal{C}_{obs}
is closed with respect to subobjects in the sense that for every
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}_{obs}
and every injection
A\hookrightarrow\iota\Omega
in
\mathcal{C}
, there must be some
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{obs}
such that
A=\iota A^{\prime}
.

The category
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
could be
\FinSet
, the sub-category of
Set
created by finite sets. In this case, when
\Context
is
Topo
,
Set
or
Meas
,
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
is a sub-category and the functor
\iota
is just inclusion. Table
1
presents several instances of Assumption
2
. An interesting instance worth special mention is as follows :

Example 1

(Pre-order valued observations)
.

An Alexandroff topology is a topology which is closed under arbitrary intersection
(
31
, e.g.)
. There is a one-to-one correspondence between Alexandroff topologies on a set
X
and preorders (reflexive and transitive relations) on
X
. The preorder naturally associated to any Alexandroff topology is called the
specialization preorder
. Here the relation
x\leq y
holds if
x
lies in the closure of
y
. One automatically obtains a subcategory
Alex
of
Topo
. There has been an increased interest in pre-order valued observations, in the form of Morse-decompositions of invariant sets
(
32
,
33
,
34
, e.g.)
. Every dynamical system or more specifically, the omega-limit set of every orbit can thus be associated with a Morse decomposition. This approach to quantifying dynamical systems corresponds to the choice of the category
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
from Assumption
2
being
Alex
.

We shall interpret a dynamical system along with a measurement as a 3-tuple
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t},\phi\right)
, where
\phi:\Omega\to\iota(\mathcal{Y})
is a morphism in
\Context
which is to be interpreted as the measurement of the domain
\Omega
. The collection of these 4-tuples also bear a categorical structure. A typical morphism in this category is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathcal{Y}^{{}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

(4)

where the blue and yellow rows represent two separate objects in
DSM
. Note that
h
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
representing a change of variables, while
A
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
, which may allow a more restricted class of morphisms. The morphisms in
DSM
thus represent change of variables that are consistent not only with the dynamics but also the measurement maps. If two measurement dynamical systems related as in (
4
), then the dataset generated by
\left(\Phi^{t},\Omega,\phi,\mathcal{Y}\right)
can be transformed into that of
\left(\Phi^{\prime t},\Omega^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\right)
.
The categorical structure borne by (
4
) is described more conveniently using the language of
comma categories
.

Comma categories

Given any category
\mathcal{C}
let
\Hom(\mathcal{C})
denote the collection of all its morphisms. A general arrangement of categories and functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

creates a special category called a
comma category

\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. Its objects are

ob\left(\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right):=\left\{\left(a,b,\phi\right)\;:\;a\in ob(\mathcal{A}),\,b\in ob(\mathcal{B}),\,\phi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\alpha a;\beta b\right)\right\},

and the morphisms comprise of pairs

\left\{(f,g)\;:\;f\in\Hom(\mathcal{A}),\,g\in\Hom(\mathcal{B})\right\}
such that the following commutation holds :

This category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
may be interpreted as connections between the functors
\alpha,\beta
, via their common codomain
\mathcal{C}
. Comma categories contain as sub-structures, the original categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, via the
forgetful
functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{2}}

whose action on morphisms in
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
can be described as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\alpha a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\beta b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\alpha a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\beta b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Comma categories prevail all over category theory and mathematics. If either
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
is taken to be
\star
the trivial category with a single object , then the resulting comma categories are called left and right
slice-categories
respectively. If
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}
, then the comma category becomes the
arrow-category
. The objects here are the arrows in
\mathcal{C}
, and the morphisms are commutation squares. Comma, slice and arrow categories thus represent finer structures present within categories.

Dynamics and observation

We have already defined the category
DS
of dynamical systems to be
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)
. We have the obvious
domain

which extracts the domain / phase-space of the dynamical system.
Now consider the arrangement

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{obs}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Context}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

The resulting comma category
\left[\dom\;;\;\iota\right]
is the category
DSM
of dynamical systems with measurement.

In all the steps of our analysis as presented in Diagram
1
, we consider different collections of objects of a particular type, along with their network of inter-relations. We have extended that to the joint concept of a dynamical system with measurement. As outlined in Table
1
, the category
\Context_{obs}
represents the particular arrangement of measurements and data-transformations that we are tasked with. The role of the functor
\iota
is to transform every observable object
\mathcal{Y}
in
\Context_{obs}
into a
\Context
object. A choice of a category determines the nature of its morphisms. The measurement
\phi
acquires the properties not of
\Context_{obs}
but of
\Context
. For that reason, it binds the domain
\Omega
not directly to
\mathcal{Y}
but to its image
\iota(\mathcal{Y})
in
\Context
.

So far we have presented categories which collectively represent all dynamical systems, and all dynamical systems along with an measurement map. We next include the notion of an orbit into this. It will be done utilizing yet another categorical construct.

4  Orbits

Table 2
:
Basic components of the framework. The table summarizes the basic ingredients of our theoretical framework. They are used to construct the various categories and functors in the diagram (
1
). See Tables
3
and
4
for more advanced constructions based on these components.

Variable

Interpretation

\Context

Context category – the collection of spaces on which we consider various dynamics, along with transformations between them. Examples are measurable spaces, vector spaces and topological spaces.

\mathcal{T}

Time category – a semigroup such as
\left(\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right)
or
\left(\mathbb{R},+\right)
which models time, represented as a 1-point category

\Context_{obs}

Category of observable space – a subcategory of
\Context
consisting of only those objects/spaces which we consider to be observable

\Delta t

Sampling functor that transforms
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
into the general time semi-group
\mathcal{T}
(Section
4
).

A full orbit in a deterministic dynamical system is completely determined by its initial point. Thus a pair of a dynamical system and an orbit is in bijective correspondence with a pair of a dynamical system and a point in it. Similarly given a dynamical system, a collection of
m
orbits corresponds to a collection of
m
initial points. In this section we discover a categorical structure within such collections. This suggests the following as the category of dynamical systems with orbits :

Definition 1
.

The category
DSO
contains as objects an ordered set of the form
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t},\omega\right)
, where
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t}\right)
is an object of
DS
, and
\omega:X\to\Omega
is some
\Context
-morphism. A morphism
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t},\omega\right)\to\left(\Omega^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime t},\omega^{\prime}\right)
is a morphism
h:\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t}\right)\to\left(\Omega^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime t}\right)
in
DS
, and a
\Context
-injection
g:X\to X^{\prime}
such that the following commutation holds

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime t}}

Thus morphisms in this category are transformations of the domain
\Omega
that preserve the initial points as well as create a semi-conjugacy.

Note that one also has a domain functor
\dom:\textbf{\text{DSO}}\to\Context
which extracts the domain object from a
DSO
object. Now that we have defined the categories
DSM
and
DSO
we can combine them into a more complex construct, via the following arrangement of functors and categories

DSO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Context_{obs}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Context}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

This leads to a comma category which we called the category of dynamics, orbits, and measurements :

\textbf{\text{DSMO}}:=\left[\dom\;;\;\iota\right]

(5)

The objects in this category is a triplet of a dynamical system
(\Omega,\Phi^{t})
, a measurement
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathcal{Y}
, and a morphism
\omega:X\to\Omega
representing a collection of initial points. Note that the first two members form an object in
DSM
. This leads to a succession of projection functors

DSM
DSMO
DS
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
DSO
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}

We have reused notation to also denote the composite functors from
DSM
and
DSO
into
\mathcal{C}
as
\dom
.
This functorial relation encodes how the objects and relations in
DS
,
DSM
and
DSMO
are contained in the other. We formally define

Definition 2

(Dynamical systems with measurements along orbits)
.

DSMO
is the category in which each object consists of a dynamical system
(\Omega,\Phi^{t})
, a measurement map
\phi:\Omega\to\iota Y
, and a morphism
\omega:X\to\Omega
. Such an object is represented as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

By Assumption
2
and the existence of epi-mono factorization, which will be explained later, we may assume
\phi
to be an epimorphism without loss of generality.

A typical morphism is a triple of morphisms
h:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
,
A:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
and an injection
g:X\to X^{\prime}
such that the following commutations hold for all times
t
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime t}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

So a morphism between two
DSMO
objects is a re-parameterization of the phase space which preserves the initial points as well as the measurements. We shall see in the next two sections that dynamical systems related this way would yield time series such that the information content of one is included in another. We have so far derived the nodes
DS
,
DSM
and
DSMO
of the figure in (
1
). The category
DSMO
may be interpreted as a comma category

There is a natural inclusion of

(6)

This inclusion thus simply adds to every measured dynamical system, the set of all its points. We have so far derived the nodes
DS
,
DSM
and
DSMO
of the figure in (
1
).

Table 3
:
Secondary set of components of the framework. The table summarizes the various categories and functors created from the building blocks declared in Table
2
. These categories and functors make a direct appearance in the diagram (
1
). See Tables
4
for more advanced constructions based on these components.

Variable

Interpretation

DS

Category of dynamical systems – a categorical interpretation of self maps on objects from the context category
\Context

\mathbb{T}

Time instants, or elements of the time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
, existing as an object of
\Context

\Seq

Category of shift spaces based in spaces selected from
\Context_{obs}

\FinSeq

Category of datasets of a fixed length, based in spaces selected from
\Context_{obs}

\TSD

Categorical interpretation of time series, in which morphisms represent the notion of information embedding

\Obs

functor
\textbf{\text{DSMO}}\to\Seq
associating to any measurement along an orbit of a dynamical system, the generated time series

\word

functor
\Seq\to\TSD
embedding any subshift into the larger and more general category of time series data

\Recon

functor
\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
that represents any general reconstruction scheme that converts time series data into dynamical systems

Discretization

Even if the time
\mathcal{T}
is a continuum, data is usually collected at discrete instants of time. This conversion of data can also be expressed functorially :

Definition 3
.

Given a semi-group category
\mathcal{T}
, a
sampling
is a functor
\Delta t:\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]\to\mathcal{T}
. Such functors are in one-to-one correspondence with semigroup homomorphisms from
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
to
\mathcal{T}
. Since the semigroup
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
is finitely generated, such a functor is uniquely defined by the image of its generating element. By a reuse of notation, this morphism in
\mathcal{T}
will also be denoted as
\Delta t
.

The sampling
\Delta t
leads to the arrangement

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Context}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

In other words, a time-
\mathcal{T}
dynamics on domain
\Context
can be converted into a discrete time set-theoretic dynamical system. In fact this pre-composition by
\Delta t
is itself a functor between functor categories, to be denoted as
\circ\Delta t
. Next note the commutation diagram below on the left :

Note that both the horizontal rows in this diagram constitute two comma categories. We show later in Lemma
11.6
in the Appendix that in any diagram of this pattern where vertical arrows connect two comma categories, there in induced functor between the comma categories. We call the induced functor
\text{Dscrt}_{\Delta t}
the
discretization
functor. Similarly we have

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(7)

By a reuse of terminology we also call this a discretization functor. If the sampling interval
\Delta t
is clear from context, it will be dropped from the subscript. It converts any dynamical system on a general mathematical space, and adherent to a general structure of time, into a discrete-time set-theoretic dynamics. Explicitly, this functor maps each
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi,\Xi)
to
(j\Omega,j\left(\Phi^{n\Delta t}\right),j\phi,j\Xi)
, where
j\Xi=\{j\omega:\omega\in\Xi\}
. Here, the domain does not have any mathematical structure other than that of a loose set of points. This is in essence, the starting point of all data-driven numerical methods.

This completes the description of yet another link in Diagram (
1
). We have provided a rigorous and categorical definition of dynamical systems, observed dynamical systems, and dynamical systems being observed along orbits. Note that none of the latter two constructs required the concept of points, sets or maps. They emerge as a category or collection of diagrams. The next items to build are the category
\Seq
and functor
\Obs
. Before that, we examine some finer properties of topological concrete categories, as established in Assumption
1
.

5  Role of the concretization functor

Recall that Assumption
1
requires a functor
U:\Context\to\textbf{\text{Set}}
. In this section, we examine the utility of this functor as well as the strong properties it provides to
\Context
. The functor
U
allows simple and intuitive set theoretic properties to be adapted for use in the more complex category
\Context
.

Epi-mono factorization

A category is said to have an epi-mono factorization if any morphism
f:a\to b
can be factorized as a composition of an monomorphism with a epimorphism, as shown below :

Moreover, this factorization must be unique up to isomorphism. In our setting of topological concrete categories, monomorphisms can be regarded as injections and epimorphisms are surjections.

Epi-mono factorizations are intuitive and commonly used in set theory as well as in linear algebra. The intermediary object is called the
image
of the morphism
f
, as indicated in the diagram above. It resides as a sub-object of
b
. One of the most useful consequences of Assumption
1
is

Property 2
.

A topological concrete category allows epi-mono factorization of morphisms, where the image of the monomorphism part by
U
is just a set theoretic inclusion.

We next investigate an even stronger form of this simple and useful property.

Any commutation between maps
\tilde{f},\tilde{q},\tilde{g},\tilde{q}
means that there as induced map between the images of
\tilde{p}
,
\tilde{q}
as shown in the right diagram as a dotted arrow. The original commutation square can be thus be factored through the more basic commutation square occupying its top half. This elementary set-theoretic observation extends to
\Context
as well :

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and we have the following two diagrams in
\Context
and
Set
respectively.

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.97205pt}{-12.42958pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.73 -17.2)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Then, there exists a unique morphism
g
such that
Ug=G
and the commutation on the right holds.

Lemma
5.1
is an example of how topological concrete categories allow set theoretic calculations to be lifted to the abstract category. The following consequence of Lemma
5.1
is of importance to us :

Lemma 5.2
.

1
holds and we have the commutative diagram in
\Context

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

then there is a morphism
g:\mathrm{im}(p)\to\mathrm{im}(q)
s.t. the two commutations on the right holds.

The epi-mono factorization property guarantees the existence of images of morphisms. The essence of Lemma
5.2
is that any commutation square factors through the images of any pair of opposing morphisms. While this property is intuitive and even trivial for
Set
, it has to be established by the diagrammatic calculus of categories for an abstract category such as in Assumption
1
. Lemma
5.2
will be used to establish the categorical notion of
orbits
.

Invariance

Take any
DSO
object
A\xrightarrow{\omega}\Omega\xrightarrow{\Phi^{t}}\Omega
and introduce the notations

\tilde{\Phi}:=U\Phi,\;\tilde{A}:=U(\omega(A)),\;\tilde{\Omega}:=U(\Omega).

to denote the usual set-theoretic description of the dynamics.
This leads to the following diagram of sets and maps in the category
Set
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{1}}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{1}+t}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{1}+t+s}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\Phi}^{t}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\Phi}^{t}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\Phi}^{t}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{2}}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{2}+t}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t_{2}+t+s}\left(\tilde{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t+s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t+s}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t+s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t+s}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{s}}

The yellow and purple rows represent two separate dynamical systems , and the vertical red arrows serve as a semi-conjugacy. By the initial lift property of topological concrete categories along with Lemma
5.2
, this leads to the creation of a semi-conjugacy of dynamics in
\Context

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}

The object
\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi)
, interpreted as the
orbit
of the sub-object
A
under the dynamics
\Phi
. is itself a sub-object or semi-dynamical system of
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t}\right)
. Intuitively,
\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi)
represents the minimal forward invariant set that contains
A
. Structurally,
\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi)
represents the minimal dynamical system that is semi-conjugate to
\left(\Omega,\Phi^{t}\right)
and contains a copy of
A
. This leads to a functor

\ForwInv:\textbf{\text{DSO}}\to\textbf{\text{DS}},\quad\left(A,\Omega,\Phi^{t}\right)\mapsto\left(\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi),\Phi^{t}\right)

(8)

One analogously has a functor

\ForwInv:\textbf{\text{DSMO}}\to\textbf{\text{DSM}},\quad\left(A,\Omega,\Phi^{t},\phi\right)\mapsto\left(\mathcal{O}(A;\Phi),\Phi^{t},\phi\right)

(9)

where we have reused the notation for simplicity. The notion of invariance enables the concept of sequence spaces, which embody both observations as well as autonomous dynamical systems.

6  Shift spaces

Shift spaces
(
13
,
7
, e.g.)
are secondary dynamical systems associated to any dynamical system with measurement. They track the dynamics induced on all possible sequences of measurements. In this section we present a purely categorical notion of subshifts, that retains all the associated notions of symbols and strings. A key tool is the existence of infinite products guaranteed by Property
1
. Given any
\mathcal{C}
-object
\mathbb{A}
one can form the infinite product
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
. This product object comes with a collection of projection morphisms indexed as
\pi^{\mathbb{N}}_{i}
as shown below in yellow :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\mathbb{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\vdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\mathbb{N}}_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\mathbb{N}}_{i_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

When
\mathcal{C}
equals
\textbf{\text{Set}},\textbf{\text{Topo}}
or
Meas
these morphisms represent the usual coordinate-wise projections. In a general category it is defined by a universal property : any collection of morphisms
\left\{f_{i}:X\to\mathbb{A}\;:\;i\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
as shown above must factor through a unique morphism
X\to\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
as shown in green above. This morphisms is suggestively labeled as the product of the
f_{i}
s. Thus a product is the universal object that encodes joint information of its factors.

Definition 4

(Shift morphism)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
. For an object
\mathbb{A}
in
\Context
, the
shift morphism
associated to
\mathbb{A}
is the unique morphism
\sigma:\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
which enables the following commuting diagram:

where
\pi^{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
is the projection to
n
-th component.

The object
\mathbb{A}
plays the role of an
alphabet
. Note that any
\Context
-object
\mathbb{A}
thus produces an domain
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
and an endomorphism
\sigma
. The resulting discrete time dynamics is precisely the
full shift-space
with alphabet
\mathbb{A}
. A finer object of a full-shift is a
subshift
:

Definition 5

(Subshift spaces)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
. Given a
\Context
-object
\mathbb{A}
, a subshift on alphabet
\mathbb{A}
is a subobject
m:S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

In the diagram above, the morphisms
m^{\prime}
and
\sigma\rvert S
are defined by the epi-mono factorization of
\sigma\circ m
. They are guaranteed to exist regardless of the nature of the morphism
m
. The extra requirement is that the injection
m^{\prime}
factorizes through
m
as shown in the upper right commuting triangle. As a result, the composite morphism created by the top horizontal row becomes a self-map on
S
, which we denote as
\sigma|S
. Note that
\sigma|S
,
\sigma
and
m
together create a semi-conjugacy as discussed earlier in Section
2
. Sub-shifts are thus sub-systems embedded within a full shift space. We next discover their own categorical structure.

Definition 6

(Category of subshifts)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
.
The subcategory of
\textbf{\text{DS}}_{\mathbb{N}_{0}}^{\Context}=\mathbb{F}\left(\mathbb{N}_{0}\;;\;\Context\right)
generated by the collection of all subshifts form a category
\Seq_{\Context}
.

If the context
\Context
is clear, we drop the subscript to denote the category simply as
\Seq
. By virtue of being a sub-category, a morphism between two subshifts
S,S^{\prime}
comprises of a
\Context
-morphism
f
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We denote the underlying set
\mathbb{A}
of object
S
by
|S|
. It is referred to as the
alphabet
of the shift space
S
. The most commonly investigated subshifts correspond to those with a finite alphabet
\mathbb{A}
. Suppose that the phase space of a dynamical system is partitioned into
\left|\mathbb{A}\right|
subsets with each piece of the partitioned indexed by some element of
\mathbb{A}
. Then each orbit in the phase space is associated with an itinerary of the partition-cells it visits. This itinerary is just an element of
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The collection of all possible itineraries is a subshift with alphabet
\mathbb{A}
.
Shift spaces are dynamical systems of their own right, situated in
\Context
. The space
\Seq
of subshifts has a finer sub-structure to it, based on the notion of decidability. Not every morphism in
\Seq
is decidable in the sense that the image can be determined by finite length observation of the sequence.

Example 2
.

Consider two objects in
\Seq_{\textbf{\text{Set}}}
: the full shift
T=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
on
2
symbols, and the subshift

S=\{\xi\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\text{ there exists }N\text{ s.t. }\xi_{k}=0\text{ for all }k\geq N\}.

We set up a map
\phi:S\to T
by

\phi(\xi)_{k}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\xi_{i}=0\text{ for all }i\geq k\\
1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Then,
\phi
is a morphism. However,
\phi(\xi)
cannot be determined by a finite observation of
\xi
.

To codify the meaning of decidability, we need the notion of
words
. To establish these notions we take note of a unique morphism
\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n}:\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}
defined by the following commutations:

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{30.65341pt}{-8.37729pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 42.42 -11.59)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq n.

Again, this projection
\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n}
is easy to decipher for Cartesian products. In a general categorical formulation, they are defined structurally by the commutations above.

Definition 7

(Words)
.

Fix an object
S
in
\Seq
and an
n\in\mathbb{N}
. consider the top horizontal row of the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n+1}}

The epi-mono factorization of the top row leads to an object labeled
\word_{n}(S)
. It is called the collection of
n
words of the subshift
S
.

Intuitively the
n
-th word function associates to each object
S
in
\Seq
the set of all finite subsequences of length
n+1
occurring in
S
.

Example 3
.

When
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
and
S
is an object in
\Seq
, then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the word functor acts as

\word_{n}(S):=\{\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}:\xi\in S\}

Definition 8

(Decidable morphisms)
.

A morphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
in
\Seq
is an
n
-morphism
if there is a morphism
f_{0}:\word_{n}(S)\to\word_{0}(S^{\prime})
such that :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{0}(S^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

The morphism
f_{0}
in this definition is determined uniquely from morphism
f
. We will call this to be the
generator
of the morphism
f
. The uniqueness follows from the surjectivity of the vertical
\word
-morphisms. The collection of
n
-morphisms for various
n
-s are called
decidable
,
finitely decidable
or simply
finite
morphisms.

Example 4
.

In a set theoretic context, a morphism
\phi:S\to T
in
\Seq
is an
n
-morphism
if the first
n+1
entries of a sequence determines the first entry of the image. Namely, if
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in S
satisfies
\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}=\xi^{\prime}_{0}\xi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\xi^{\prime}_{n}
, then we have
\phi(\xi)_{0}=\phi(\xi^{\prime})_{0}
.

Example 5
.

1.

The identity map
\Id_{S}:S\to S
is a 0-morphism.

2.

The shift map
\sigma:S\to S
is a 1-morphism.

3.

An
n
-morphism is an
m
-morphism for all
m\geq n
.

Data processing procedures, such as moving average or difference, can be formulated as finite morphisms. To give an explicit description of the structure of
n
-morphisms, we need the following observation.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
f:S\to S^{\prime}
be an
n
-morphism, and
f_{0}
its generator. Then, we have

(Uf)(\xi)_{i}=(Uf_{0})\left(\xi_{i}\xi_{i+1}\cdots\xi_{i+n}\right),\quad\forall\xi\in U(S),\;i\in\mathbb{N}.

Lemma
6.1
is proved in Section
11.6
in the Appendix. The image under
U
of any subshift is also a set-theoretic subshift. Lemma
6.1
provides an explicit formula for the generator of this subshift.

Terminal object

A terminal object
\bar{x}
in a category
\mathcal{X}
has the property that for any object
x
of
\mathcal{X}
, there is a unique morphism
x\to\bar{x}
. A terminal object is usually denoted as
1_{\mathcal{X}}
. The right slice category
\left[1_{\mathcal{X}}\;;\;\mathcal{X}\right]
of
1_{\mathcal{X}}
will be called the category of
pointed objects
of
\mathcal{X}
. The objects are morphisms
a:1_{\mathcal{X}}\to A
, and a morphism between any two such objects
a:1_{\mathcal{X}}\to A
and
a^{\prime}:1_{\mathcal{X}}\to A^{\prime}
is a morphism
\phi:A\to A^{\prime}
such that
\phi\circ a=a^{\prime}
. Note that each object of
\left[1_{\mathcal{X}}\;;\;\mathcal{X}\right]
requires a choice of an object
A
, followed by the choice of a morphism
a:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to A
. The latter is interpreted as a point in the object
a
, hence the name. Fortunately, such an object is guaranteed to us :

Property 3
.

A category
\Context
satisfying Assumption
1
has a terminal object
1_{\Context}
.

Definition 9

(Strings)
.

Let
S
be a subshift on alphabet
\mathbb{A}
. A
string
of length
n+1
is a morphism of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

Example 6
.

When
\Context
is either
\textbf{\text{Set}},\textbf{\text{Topo}}
or
Meas
, any morphism originating from the terminal object represents a point. In these context, a string fits the usual definition of being a point in a collection of words.

Definition 10

(Cylinders)
.

Let
S
be a subshift on alphabet
\mathbb{A}
, and
1_{\Context}\xrightarrow{w}\word_{n}(S)
be a string. Then the cylinder around the string
w
is the unique object
\Cylinder(w)
created from the following pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cylinder(w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}}

Example 7
.

When
\Context
is either
\textbf{\text{Set}},\textbf{\text{Topo}}
or
Meas
, the cylinder
\Cylinder(w)
denotes the subset or subspace of
S
whose first
n+1
symbols coincide with the symbols in
w
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let Assumption
1
hold. Then for every string
w
, the cylinder
\Cylinder(w)
is a subobject of
S
.

Lemma
6.2
is proved in Section
11.6
in the Appendix.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
f:S\to S^{\prime}
be an
n
-morphism. Then, we have a family of morphisms
f^{(m)}:\word_{n+m}(S)\to\word_{m}(S^{\prime})
indexed by
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following commutations hold :

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Lemma
6.3
is proved in Section
11.6
in the Appendix. Another important observation to be made is that finite morphisms are closed under composition :

Lemma 6.4
.

The composition of an
n
-morphism with an
m
-morphism produces an
(m+n)
-morphism.

Lemma
6.4
is proved in Section
11.6
in the Appendix. The proof of Lemma
6.4
explicitly provides the generator for the morphism, whose law of generation is given in (
11
). This statement can be expanded as

Lemma 6.5
.

Given a
m
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
and an
l
-morphism
\phi^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime\prime}
,
\phi
, one has

(\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi)^{(m)}=\phi^{\prime(m)}\circ\phi^{(m+l)}.

(10)

Lemma
6.5
is a direct consequence of Lemma
6.3
. Lemma
6.3
lays down the composition rule for the finite versions of subshift morphisms.

Example 8
.

Again consider the case when
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
. Given a morphism
\phi
, let
\hat{\phi}=\pi_{0}\circ\phi
denote the projection to its first coordinate. Thus,
\phi
is an
n
-morphism if
\hat{\phi}
is constant on all
n
-cylinders. A morphism must commute with the shift map. Thus we must have

\pi_{k}\circ\phi=\pi_{0}\circ\sigma^{k}\circ\phi=\pi_{0}\circ\phi\circ\sigma^{k}=\hat{\phi}\circ\sigma^{k},

for every index
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Thus finitely defined morphisms between shift spaces are completely defined by their first coordinate-projections. Given any
n
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
, one thus has an induced map

\phi^{(m)}_{*}:\word_{m+n}(S)\to\word_{m}(S^{\prime}),\quad\left(s_{0},\ldots,s_{m+n}\right)\mapsto\left(s^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{m}\right),\;s^{\prime}_{j}:=\hat{\phi}\left(s_{j},\ldots,s_{j+n}\right),

(11)

for every index
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This map
\hat{\phi}
will be called the
generator
for the morphism
\phi
.

Example 9
.

Consider the case when
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
. If
\phi:S\to T
is an
n
-morphism and
\psi:T\to Y
is an
m
-morphism, then
\psi\phi:S\to Y
is an
m+n
-morphism. We can check that the generator of the morphism
\psi\phi
is

\left(s_{0},\ldots,s_{m+n}\right)\,\mapsto\,\hat{\psi}\left(\hat{\phi}\left(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}\right),\ldots,\hat{\phi}\left(s_{m},\ldots,s_{m+n}\right)\right).

Lemma
6.4
leads to the following discovery of categorical structure.

Theorem 1
.

Finite morphisms define a subcategory
\Seq_{<\infty}
of
\Seq
. Similarly, 0-morphisms define a subcategory
\Seq_{0}
of
\Seq
.

We have thus progressed to another node
\Seq
from Diagram (
1
). The objects here are quite distant from the
DSMO
objects from where they may originate. A data-driven algorithm only has available, or finite sets or sequences of data. We later encapsulate their properties in a category labeled
\TSD
. There is no pre-existing information of an explicit dynamics within the data, or of a topological or measure space. Objects in
\Seq
are midway between the objects in
DSMO
and
\TSD
. A
\Seq
object is collection of infinitely long records of measurements on the original space. At the same time, they also have an in-built dynamics due to the shift map.

7  Measurement along orbits

We now come to the realization that shift spaces arise naturally from dynamical systems with measurements. In the previous section we arrived at a purely categorical understanding of Cartesian products
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Such products naturally arise when a dynamical system is being observed. An observation
\phi:\Omega\to\iota Y
involves an object
Y
from
\Context_{obs}
. The sequences of observations at intervals of
\Delta t
naturally lead to sequences in
(\iota Y)^{\mathbb{N}}
. We begin by studying some fundamental connections of this phase with the original domain.

Our first observation is that For each object
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)
in
DSM
, we have a morphism
\xi_{(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)}:\Omega\to(\iota Y)^{\mathbb{N}}
defined by the commutations

\hbox to148.37pt{\vbox to51.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 74.18513pt\lower-25.19226pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(12)

The left-diagram shows how the dynamics
\Phi
, the measurements
\phi\circ\Phi^{n}
along the orbits, and shift on the symbol space
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbb{N}}
and the projection maps commute. Lemma
5.2
may be applied to the peripheral commuting square of this diagram to get the commutative diagram in the middle. Thus induced endomorphism on the image of
\xi
has also been denoted as
\sigma
. Thus we get a dynamical system which is subobject or sub-system of the full shift. This can be summarized as the existence of a functor
\Obs
as shown on the right above. One can perform the same steps with
DSMO
. Recalling the notation from (
9
) we can draw :

(13)

We now formally state the functor property of these correspondences
\Obs
:

Theorem 2

(Observation functor)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then the correspondences shown in (
12
) and (
13
) are functorial.

Theorem
2
is proved in Section
11.7
in the Appendix, with the help of Lemma
5.1
. The functor
\Obs
thus describes a systematic way in which measurements along orbits is converted into sequences, and how semiconjugacy relations are also preserved as subshift maps. In fact, the morphisms in
\textbf{\text{DSMO}}^{\mathbb{N}}
are converted into
0
-morphisms in
\Seq
. The objects in
\Seq
are a record of all possible sequences of measurements one can obtain from the
DSMO
object.

Example 10
.

\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
, the action of the functor

A typical morphism as in (
4
) induces the following
\Seq
-morphism :

\left\{\phi\left(\Phi^{n+n_{0}}(x)\right)\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\mapsto\left\{A\circ\phi\left(\Phi^{n+n_{0}}(x)\right)\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}=\left\{\phi^{\prime}\left(\Phi^{\prime n+n_{0}}(h\left(x\right))\right)\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}.

This completes the description of yet another link in Diagram (
1
).

Since
\Seq
is a subcategory of
\textbf{\text{DS}}^{\mathbb{N}}
there is an obvious inclusion functor :

\PathF:\Seq\to\mathbb{F}\left(\mathbb{N}_{0}\;;\;\textbf{\text{Set}}\right)=\textbf{\text{DS}}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}.

which assigns to any subshift
S\subset\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
the dynamical system
(S,\sigma)
. We have talked about measurements over an entire orbit or trajectory. We have represented an orbit by its initial state, thereby providing an indirect characterization of orbits. We now have the ingredients for a direct description of the notion of an orbit. Intuitively, an orbit of a point is the smallest dynamical system that contains all the forward iterates of that point. This is formalized by the
orbit
functor
\mathcal{O}
shown below as a dashed arrow :

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DSMO}}^{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DS}}^{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Seq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Dscrt}_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PathF}

(14)

With all the concepts in place, we can proceed to define the final category of objects of Diagram (
1
) - time series data. The concept of sampling and subshifts will play an important role.

8  Time series data

Data-driven reconstruction algorithms abound in various fields of dynamical systems. The overall goal is to create a mathematical object from some collection
\mathcal{C}
of data. To compare different techniques or to evaluate a particular technique, one needs a metric or means of comparison between the members of
\mathcal{C}
. In our case the collection is
DS
and comparison is provided by its categorical structure. The other ingredient needed is way to model data itself as a mathematical object. While the nature of data can be very diverse, there are some basic principles to keep in mind when defining data as a mathematical object.

1.

All algorithms on a finite memory machine must receive finite datasets as inputs.

2.

Different algorithms may receive different data-sets and thus the comparison should not be based on a particular choice of dataset.

3.

The performance of an algorithm should be expected to improve with an increase in dataset. Thus an algorithm should be evaluated by its limiting results as the dataset increases in information.

4.

Thus the definition of a dataset must allow a rigorous notion of increase in information or content.

5.

The definition of a dataset must be able to incorporate the increasing information content in time series.

We begin by defining finitely sized data set objects :

Definition 11

(Category of finite sequences)
.

This is the category
\FinSeq
in which objects are triples
(\mathbb{A},n,X)
with
\mathbb{A}
being a
\mathcal{C}
-object,
n
an integer, and
X
a sub-object of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
. A morphism between two such objects
(\mathbb{A},n,X)
and
(\mathbb{A}^{\prime},n^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is an usual
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
X\to X^{\prime}
.

Any such subobject
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}
will be denoted as a triple
(n,\mathbb{A},X)
or more simply just as
X
. The integer
n
will be called the
length
of the finite sequence, and is represented as
\len X
. The set
\mathbb{A}
is interpreted as the
alphabet
of
X
, and is represented as
|X|
, and the integer
n
. A
\FinSeq
-object represents a chunk of data, one of the simplest units of information. Any numerical method operates on one such data chunk at a time. An infinite sequence of these chunks create a time-series object.

Definition 12

(Start and finish morphisms)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
and
\mathbb{A}
be an object in
\Context
. For each
n
, there are morphisms
\eta_{n}:\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{n}:\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
defined by the following diagrams:

\hbox to146.43pt{\vbox to48.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 73.21661pt\lower-24.00609pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-73.21661pt}{-20.34636pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -101.31 -28.15)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
