\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}};\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq n-1

These morphisms
\eta_{n}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
will be called the
start
and
finish
morphisms respectively.

The symbols
\eta
and
\mathfrak{f}
respectively represent the act of deleting the starting symbol, and final symbol. The simple ideas of a start and finish morphism are the basis of the notion of data.

Definition 13

(Time series data object)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
. A
\Context
-valued time series data comprises of (i) an alphabet
\mathbb{A}\in\Context
; (ii) a sequence
\{X_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
of
\Context
-objects; (iii) and morphisms
\{\eta_{n},\mathfrak{f}_{n}:X_{n+1}\to X_{n}\}_{n}
such that

1.

X_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n+1}
:
X_{n}
is a finite sequence with alphabet
\mathbb{A}
and length
n+1
.

2.

The following commutations hold

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n},\mathfrak{f}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n+1},\mathfrak{f}_{n+1}}

Note the repeat of notations in the two horizontal rows. They convey the intuition that the morphisms
\eta_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i-1}
which are a restriction of start and finish morphisms on the full symbolic space
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}
. Thus intuitively, a time series data consists of a sequence
X=\{X_{i}\}_{i}
(
i=0,1,2,\cdots
) of objects in
\FinSeq
with a common alphabet
\mathbb{A}
and a collection of start and finish morphisms representing data deletion.

Example 11
.

When
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
, then

\eta_{i}(x_{0}x_{1}\cdots x_{i})=x_{1}\cdots x_{i},\quad\mathfrak{f}_{i}(x_{0}x_{1}\cdots x_{i})=x_{0}x_{1}\cdots x_{i-1}.

We represent a time series data graphically as

\begin{diagram}

(15)

We allow a time series data to be finite length. That is, we may have
X_{k}=\emptyset
for all
k
greater than some integer
N
. Such time series data will be called
finite
. For convenience, we define
\eta_{0}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}:X_{0}\to X_{-1}=\{\varepsilon\}
where
\varepsilon
denotes the null sequence. A time series object is thus a sequence of data-chunks of increasing length. The portion of the
(n+1)
-length chunks obtained by deleting the beginning and ending symbols, must be within the
n
-length chunks.

To define the time series data as objects of a category we need to define morphisms.

Definition 14

(Category of time series data)
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
. Then time series data objects assemble to form a category
\TSD_{\Context}
in which a morphism between two time series data
X
and
Y
is an integer
k
along with a sequence of maps
\psi_{n}:X_{n+k}\to Y_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following commutations hold :

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.70251pt}{-24.40688pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.97 -33.77)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

(16)

The composition is defined in an obvious manner.

As usual, if the context is clear, then
\TSD_{\Context}
will denoted more briefly as
\TSD
. The vertical columns in (
16
) represent two separate objects in
\TSD
, while the horizontal arrows represent the
n
and
n+1
-th component of a morphism between them. This integer
k
will be called the
jump
of the morphism. The verification of compositionality is routine and is provided in Section
11.8
in the appendix.

The category
\TSD_{\textbf{\text{Set}}}
so defined turns out to be a special case of a functor category used to capture the notion of increasing information content. Following
(
35
, Sec 7)
, we may interpret the sequence in (
15
) to be a sequence of probability spaces, and the pair of morphisms
\left(\eta_{n},\mathfrak{f}_{n}\right)
create a Markov transition function. Each point
w
in
X_{n}
is sent with equal probability to
\eta_{n}(w)
or
\mathfrak{f}_{n}(w)
. The sequence represents an increasing information content, and the Markov transition function represents a lossy projection map. In this way,
\TSD_{\textbf{\text{Set}}}
becomes a sub-category of the category of functors from
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},\downarrow\right]
into
Stoch
, the category of stochastic processes
(
36
, e.g.)
. Such sequences are the object of study in Lauritzen’s notion of transitivity of a sequence of statistics
(
37
, Def 2.1)
.Thus each object in
\TSD_{\textbf{\text{Set}}}
is a sequence of increasing information content, and the transition from the
n+1
-th piece to the
n
-th piece is a Markov transition in which either the first or last bit of a sequence is lost.

We next make some observations about the generation of morphisms in
\TSD
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be time series data, and (
16
) holds for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then
\psi_{n+1}
is completely determined by
\psi_{n}
.

Lemma
8.1
is proved in Section
11.8
of the appendix.

Example 12
.

If
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
then Lemma
8.1
can be expressed by the identity

\phi_{n+1}(\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n+k+1})=\phi_{n}(\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n+k})_{0}\phi_{n}(\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n+k+1}),\quad\forall\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n+k+1}\in X_{n+k+1}.

According to Lemma
8.1
the morphisms in
\TSD
of jump 0 are in one-to-one correspondence with the set maps which generate them. For this reason, we shall denote each
0
-morphism in
\TSD
by
X\xrightarrow{k}Y
if its generating map is
k:|X|\to|Y|
. This completes the description of the rightmost node of the diagram in (
1
).

Time series from subshifts

We have seen how objects in
\Seq
arise naturally from dynamical systems with measurements. We shall now see how
\TSD
-objects are naturally associated with
\Seq
-objects. Consider the commutative diagram in
\Context
shown below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{n+1}}

This leads to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{n+1}}

(17)

Lemma
5.2
can be applied to this diagram to get the diagram on the right. The
n
-th word associated to a subshift is thus the image of its projection to the first
n+1
components. This can be formalized as

Theorem 3
.

There is a functor
\word:\Seq_{\Context,<\infty}\to\TSD
set up by
\word(S)_{n}:=\word_{n}(S)
as described in (
17
).

We have already established the action of this functor on objects. The verification of compositionality is done in Section
11.8
.

Example 13
.

When
\Context=\textbf{\text{Set}}
then

\word_{n}:\Seq\to\textbf{\text{Set}},\quad S\mapsto\left\{\mbox{ all }n-\mbox{strings in }S\right\}.

We have thus described another node and segment of the outline diagram (
1
). The
\word
-functor (
17
), discretization functor
Dscrt
(
7
), and observation functor
\Obs
(Theorem
2
) together compose into the
data
functor

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DSMO}}_{\mathbb{N},\text{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Seq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Data_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Dscrt}_{\Delta t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word}

(18)

The diagram (
18
) breaks down the process by which an observed dynamical system generates data. The data is in the form of time series objects, which are obtained as words or strings from shift spaces created from the dynamics. Observed dynamics are arranged by commuting relations as shown in (
4
). These relations are transformed into semi-conjugacy relations in
\Seq
, which in turn are converted into the commutations of the form (
16
). As usual
\Delta t
may be dropped from the subscript if it is clear from context.

We now present several instances of
DSMO
objects to illustrate our results. In all these examples, time
\mathcal{T}
is
\mathbb{N}_{0}
by default.

Example 14

(Circle rotation 1)
.

Suppose
\Omega=S^{1}
and the dynamics is

\Phi:S^{1}\to S^{1},\quad\theta\mapsto\theta+\rho\bmod 2\pi

Suppose that the system is being observed along a single trajectory starting from
A=\left\{(0,0)\right\}
. Next suppose that the observation map is

\phi:S^{1}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\theta\mapsto\cos(\theta).

Then the resulting time series data
X
will be such that each
X_{N}
comprises of sequences of length
N+1
of the form
\left\{\cos((n_{0}+n)\rho\Delta t\right\}_{n=0}^{N}
for various
n_{0}
. Any trigonometric reconstruction scheme would produce an exact reconstruction.

Example 15

(Circle rotation 2)
.

Consider the same setup as Example
14
but with
A
instead being the entirety of
S^{1}
. Then the resulting time series data
X
will be such that each
X_{N}
comprises of sequences of length
N+1
which are linear combinations of trigonometric functions whose frequencies are multiples of
\rho\Delta t
. Any trigonometric reconstruction scheme would produce an exact reconstruction.

Example 16

(Quasiperiodic torus rotation 1)
.

Suppose
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the 2-dimensional torus, and the dynamics is

\Phi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad\theta\mapsto\theta+\rho\bmod 2\pi.

Here
\theta
is a 2-dimensional angular coordinate, and
\rho
is a 2-dimensional rotation vector. If the coordinates of
\rho
are rationally independent, then the motion is known as
quasiperiodic
. Suppose that the set of initial conditions
A
is the entirety of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
\phi
is an embedding of then the resulting time series data
X
will be such that each
X_{N}
comprises of sequences of length
N+1
which are linear combinations of trigonometric functions whose frequencies are of the form
\left\{a_{1}\rho_{1}\Delta t+a_{2}\rho_{2}\Delta t\;:\;a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
. Again, any trigonometric reconstruction scheme would produce an exact reconstruction.

Example 17

(Quasiperiodic torus rotation 2)
.

Consider the variant of Example
16
above in which the rotation vector
\rho
is such that there is an
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
a\cdot\rho\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then there is another
b\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that the resulting time series data
X
will be such that each
X_{N}
comprises of sequences of length
N+1
which are linear combinations of trigonometric functions whose frequencies multiples of
(b\cdot\rho)\Delta t
. Thus the time series will only bear the signature of a single generating frequency. No reconstruction scheme would be able to produce an exact reconstruction.

Example 18

(Quasiperiodic torus rotation 3)
.

Consider the variant of Example
16
above in which the observation map is

\phi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\theta\mapsto\cos(b\cdot\theta),

for some
b\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then the resulting time series data
X
will be such that each
X_{N}
comprises of sequences of length
N+1
which are linear combinations of trigonometric functions whose frequencies multiples of
(b\cdot\rho)\Delta t
. Again, no reconstruction scheme would be able to produce an exact reconstruction.

An important point to note is that the
DSMO
objects from Examples
14
,
17
and
18
produce the same time series if the parameters are chosen correctly. This is one of the simplest examples of the challenge or impossibility of reconstructing a
DS
-object from a
\TSD
-object which is sourced not from
DS
but from
DSMO
.

We have thus established the entire path in Diagram (
1
) that leads to a generation of data from a dynamical system being measured along orbits. This sets the stage to consider the task of reconstruction.

9  Reconstruction

Table 4
:
Tertiary set of components of the framework. The table summarizes the various categories and functors derived from the categories and functors in Table
3
, and ultimately depending on
2
. These categories and functors are the key to make conclusions from the arrangement in diagram (
1
).

Variable

Interpretation

DSM

Category of dynamical systems along with measurement maps into objects from the observable category
\Context_{obs}

DSMO

Category of dynamical systems along with measurement maps and an orbit

\Data

functor
\textbf{\text{DSMO}}\to\TSD
resulting from the composition of
\word
with
\Obs
. Represents how an observation system leads to the creation of a time series object.

\bar{\Recon}

functor
\textbf{\text{DSMO}}\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
resulting from the composition of
\Recon
with
\Data
. Represents how an observation system leads to a reconstructed dynamical system, depending on the choice of reconstruction scheme
\Recon
.

Our study has two parts. The first is about the separate notions of dynamical systems, observations and orbits, and how they combine to provide data. The notion of ”data” is formalized as objects of a category called time series-data
\TSD
. One of our main conclusions has been that all these arrangements including
\TSD
are related functorially. Data-driven reconstruction algorithms operate on time series to create dynamical systems, in an effort to estimate the source of the data. The second part of our investigation explores the consequences of these algorithms being functorial. Before delving into the categorical math we must clarify the assumption on functoriality.

Any algorithm is ultimately a function
\mathcal{A}
, that accepts instances of certain data-types and provides an output of some other data-type. The output also depends upon some internal parameters. Some of the parameters represent design choices, while some represent unavoidable limitations of computer based calculations. For example all learning algorithms have a choice of hypothesis space
\mathcal{H}
which is preferably a dense subset of the space in which the target function lies. During any computation one can only employ a finite subspace of
\mathcal{H}
. This subspace may be parameterized in different ways, such as the length and breadth of a neural network, the number of basis or frame elements in a regression technique. In either case, there is a number
L
which represents the size of the actual search space. There are also other parameters involved, such as a
machine precision
limit
\epsilon_{mach}
which represents the resolution of computer arithmetic. All these intrinsic parameters related to performance can be bundled into a variable
\lambda
drawn from a space
\Lambda
. Based on the notation so far, an algorithm can be expressed as a map :

\mathcal{A}:\FinSeq\times\Lambda\to\textbf{\text{DS}}.

We shall assume that the parameter space
\Lambda
has a point at infinity
\bar{\lambda}
, representing the limiting or terminal value of the parameter variable. The function

\bar{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\bar{\lambda}):\FinSeq\to\textbf{\text{DS}}

represents the idealized performance of the algorithm beyond all of its performance limiting factors. For example in a least-squares fit approach, given a dataset
X_{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following steps are are followed :

1.

Construct an augmented space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
using techniques such as delay-coordinates or reservoir embedding. Without loss of generality, the measurement function can be interpreted directly as an injective map
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
.

2.

The dataset
X_{N}
is some collection of sequences of length
N+1
of orbits in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.

3.

Take a gradation
\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\ldots
of the hypothesis space into a sequence of finite dimensional subspace.

4.

Fix a search size
L
.

5.

Find a minimum norm function
w_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
from
\mathcal{H}_{L}
such that the graph of
w_{N}
has the least-squares fit with the length
N+1
time series contained in
X_{N}
. As a result the output
\mathcal{A}(X_{N},L)
will be
\left(\mathbb{R}^{D},w_{N}\right)
.

6.

The output
\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X_{N})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{D},\bar{w}_{N}\right)
is such that the minimum norm function
\bar{w}_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
such that the graph of
\Phi
has the least-squares fit with the length
N+1
time series contained in
X_{N}
.

This is where the assumption of functoriality plays a natural role. Datasets can undergo various transformation such as scaling, rotations, and translations. Categorically, two datasets
X_{N},X^{\prime}_{N}
of length
N
may be related by a morphism in
\FinSeq
,
h:X_{N}\to X^{\prime}_{N}
. We want the idealized algorithm
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
to be compatible with such data transformations. This means that if the reconstructions are respectively

(\Omega,\Phi^{t})=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X_{N}),(\Omega^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime t})=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime}_{N}),

then the two dynamics should be related by a semi-conjugacy too. An algorithm
\mathcal{A}
usually places a regularity bound such as
C^{1}
norm or total variation norm on the candidates in its hypothesis space. Thus it might fail to preserve semi-conjugacy under some distortions of the data. On the other hand
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
does not place regularity bounds and does not lose information content under data transformations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Limiting}\\
\mbox{parameter }\\
\bar{\lambda}\end{array}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Parameter : }\\
\lambda\in\Lambda\end{array}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Input : }\\
X_{N}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Input : }\\
X_{N}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\mathcal{A}\left(X_{N},\lambda\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X_{N+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Input : }\\
X_{N+1}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Input : }\\
X_{N+1}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\mathcal{A}\left(X_{N+1},\lambda\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\cdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{TSD}\\
X\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{TSD}\\
X\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\Recon(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\to\bar{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\to\bar{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Figure 4
:
Algorithms and their limiting behavior. A
\TSD
-object (
15
)
X
is represented as a sequence of sets of
N
-sequences
X_{N}
. Each such dataset
X_{N}
is mapped by the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
into an estimate of the dynamical system. This mapping depends on the state of the design and performance parameters
\lambda
. Their limiting behavior is indicated in blue by
\bar{A}
. Although
\mathcal{A}
is not functorial,
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
is. In topological reconstructions, by pointwise convergence, the limit
\bar{A}(X)
of the estimates
\bar{A}(X_{N})
have a functorial dependence on
X
. The reconstruction functor
\Recon(X)
is precisely this limit. It does not represent the output of an algorithm but its idealized operation.

We next explain why the successive approximations
\left\{\bar{\mathcal{A}(X_{N})}\right\}_{N=1,2,\ldots}
converge to a limit which we denote as
\bar{A}(X)
. This limit will be interpreted as the final, ideal reconstruction
\Recon(X)
. Recall from the definition of the
\TSD
category that each
X_{N}
is a collection of strings of an alphabet
\mathbb{A}
, all the strings being of length
N+1
. There is also a pair of deletion maps
\eta_{N},\mathfrak{f}_{N}:X_{N}\to X_{N-1}
. The implication is that
X_{N-1}
contains every
N
-length substring that can be obtained by deleting the starting or ending symbol in an
N+1
-length string in
X_{N}
. The converse may not be true, i.e., there may be strings in
X_{N-1}
not in the image of
\eta_{N},\mathfrak{f}_{N}
. Let
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0}\,;\,\mathbb{A}\right]_{\text{int}}
denote the set of all possible infinite sequences from the alphabet
\mathbb{A}
. This is also called the
path-space
of
\mathbb{A}
. One can associate a subset
\mathcal{S}_{N}
of
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0}\,;\,\mathbb{A}\right]_{\text{int}}
corresponding to
X_{N}
:

\mathcal{S}_{N}:=\left\{\phi:\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{A}\;:\;\phi(0)\phi(1)\cdots\phi(N)\in X_{N}\right\}.

Our previous observation implies that these sets are decreasing :
\mathcal{S}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{S}_{2}\supseteq\cdots
. Moreover, if
\mathbb{A}
is a compact set, then these form a sequence of nested compact sets, whose intersection
\mathcal{S}
is therefore non-empty and compact. Thus any
\TSD
-object
X
unambiguously determines a compact subset
\mathcal{S}
of the path-space of
\mathbb{A}
.

Therefore as
N
is increased, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is fed increasing amount of information about this subset
\mathcal{S}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is a consistent reconstruction method, then its approximations should converge to a final estimate which replicates the sequences in
\mathcal{S}
. This final, limiting estimate is precisely what is indicated by
\bar{\mathcal{A}}(X)
or
\Recon(X)
. The correspondence
X\mapsto\Recon(X)
is also functorial. For simplicity we assume that at each stage the domain of the reconstruction remains the augmented space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Suppose that there is a jump
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence of maps
h_{N}:X_{N}\to X_{N+k}
which commutes with the start and finish morphisms. Our assumption on data-compatibility implies the following commutation for every
N
:

Any dynamical system is equivalent to a conjugate form involving an invertible change of variables. Thus without loss of generality we may assume that the two dynamics have two reference points
x_{0}
and
x^{\prime}_{0}
, and
h_{N}(x_{0})=x^{\prime}_{0}
at every stage
N
. Then consistency of the algorithm implies that the
h_{N}
converges pointwise on the closure of the orbit of
x_{0}
. Our observations have been summarized in Figure
4
. Thus we define

Definition 15

(Reconstruction scheme)
.

A data-driven reconstruction scheme for dynamical systems is a functor
\Recon:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
.

If a semi-conjugacy relation is invertible, it is called a
conjugacy
. A dynamical system is only unique up to conjugacy. Conjugacies correspond to isomorphisms in the category
DS
. By Theorem
2
, we have a functor
\word\circ\Obs
(by an abuse of notation) from
DSM
to
\TSD
constructed by assigning the set of all data generated by a specific measurement and a dynamical system. Therefore, two conjugate systems will yield datasets isomorphic in
\TSD
. Like many constructs in category theory, the output of any reconstruction scheme should be considered along with all its conjugate forms. This definition of reconstruction completes the last link in the diagram (
1
). We have thus arrived at the following situation :

DSM
DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DSMO}}_{\mathbb{N},\text{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Seq}
DS
DS
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj}
Dscrt
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Data}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}

(19)

The left loop extracts the dynamical system from a combined observation + dynamics + orbit object. It can be interpreted as the ”true” dynamical system. A data-consistent reconstruction algorithm is a functor shown as the dotted red arrow above, such that the two composite functors shown in dashed green arrows are comparable. The question that now arises is how can two paths be reconciled.

10  Consistency

The reconstruction
\Recon
only takes measured data as inputs, since the phase space cannot be observed directly. However, if the data is generated from a full observation of the phase space
\Omega
, then one should ideally be able to fully reconstruct the system. For this purpose we define a subcategory
\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}
of
DS
in which the domains are images of the inclusion functor
\iota
from Assumption
2
. There is an inclusion
\iota^{\prime}_{obs}:\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}\to\textbf{\text{DSM}}
, given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{\iota\Omega}}

There are also analogous subcategories
\textbf{\text{DSO}}_{obs}
of
DSO
and
\textbf{\text{DSMO}}_{obs}
of
DSMO
in which the dynamics is on an observable domain. Thus each of the three categories
DS
,
DSM
,
DSMO
have their observable analogs, and these six categories are organized as follows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}}

Note how the various inclusion functors
\iota_{obs}
,
\iota^{\prime}_{obs}
,
\iota^{\prime\prime}_{obs}
, and projections
\proj
and
\proj_{obs}
combine with the inclusion functor
\kappa
from (
6
). Some of the functors are left and right inverses, as encoded in the commutation loops involving equalities. Of special significance is the arrow
\iota_{obs}=\kappa\circ\iota^{\prime}_{obs}:\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
. This functor maps each
(\iota\Omega,\Phi)
in
\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}
to
\left(\iota\Omega,\Phi,\Id_{\iota\Omega},\Id_{\iota\Omega}\right)
. We are now ready to delve into the various notions related to reconstruction, centered around the Diagram (
19
).

Definition 16

(Consistency)
.

A reconstruction
\Recon
as in Diagram (
19
) will be called
observation-consistent
if there is a natural transformation
\Forget\circ\iota_{obs}\Rightarrow\Recon\circ\Data\circ\iota_{obs}
, as shown below

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime\prime}_{obs}}

Thus consistency implies that the construction functor applied to a data-object
X
arising from a fully observed, observable dynamical system would produce a new data-object
X^{\prime}
which may not be isomorphic to
X
but is derivable from
X
. One can define more generally :

Definition 17

(Parameterized Consistency)
.

Suppose there is a functor
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}

\Recon
as in Diagram (
19
) will be called
consistent
with respect to
F
, or simple
F
-consistent, if there is a natural transformation
\Forget\circ F\Rightarrow\Recon\circ\Data\circ F
, as shown below

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Note how observation-based consistency is a special case of parameterized consistency. Changing the direction of the natural transformation leads to :

Definition 18

(Simulator)
.

A reconstruction
\Recon
as in Diagram (
19
) will be called
observation-simulator
if there is a natural transformation
\Recon\circ\Data\circ\iota_{obs}\Rightarrow\Forget\circ\iota_{obs}
, as shown below

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{\text{DS}}_{obs}}
DS
DS

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime\prime}_{obs}}

Thus being a observation simulator means that if the construction functor is applied to a data-object
X
arising from a fully observed, observable dynamical system, the new data-object
X^{\prime}
it produces may not be isomorphic to
X
but can be transformed into
X
. One can define more generally :

Definition 19

19
) will be called

with respect to
F
, or simple

-consistent, if there is a natural transformation
\Recon\circ\Data\circ F\Rightarrow\Forget\circ F
, as shown below

One can ask for a stronger property of reconstruction algorithms, to yield exact reconstructions. This suggests the following natural property :

.

A reconstruction

) will be called

if the isomorphism on the left holds :

(21)

Note that if two dynamical systems are related by an invertible change of variables, then their data will have the exact information content. Thus reconstruction and consistency can be exact only up to isomorphisms. The diagram on the right expresses this equivalence of functors via a commutation diagram. The two sides of the equality correspond to the two composite functors created by the clockwise and counter-clockwise paths. We define similarly :

Definition 21

(Generalized exact reconstruction)
.

Given any functor
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
, a reconstruction is said to be
exact
w.r.t.
F
if
\Recon\circ\Data\circ F\cong\Forget\circ F
. In other words there is a functor
G:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
creating the following commutative diagram :

DSMO
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TSD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
DS
DS
DS
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Data}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Recon}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(22)

This completes a list of several properties of reconstructions based on the relations
\Recon
might try to achieve. One can also characterize reconstructions by their methodology. They mainly fall under two types -
inner
and
outer
approximations.

Definition 22

(Observation based inner / outer approximation)
.

A reconstruction
\Recon
as in Diagram (
19
) will be called an
observation-based
inner approximation if for every
\TSD
-object
X
,
\Recon(X)
is the colimit of all those observable dynamical systems
(\Omega,\Phi)
for which there a
DSMO
version
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi,A)
whose data
Y:=\Data\left(\Omega,\Phi,\phi,A\right)
transforms into
X
. Similarly,
\Recon
will be called an observation-based outer approximation
\Recon(X)
is the limit of all those observable dynamical systems
(\Omega,\Phi)
for which there a
DSMO
version
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi,A)
whose data
Y:=\Data\left(\Omega,\Phi,\phi,A\right)
can be transformed from
X
.

An inner approximation
\Recon(X)
looks for the closest match to the data
X
from the left, while an outer approximation looks for the closest match from the right. They are analogous to the notions of greatest lower bounds and least upper bounds. One can define more generally :

Definition 23

(Generalized inner-outer approximation)
.

Suppose there is a functor
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
, interpretable as a parameterized sub-family of dynamical systems being measured along orbits. A reconstruction
\Recon
as in Diagram (
19
) will be called an inner approximation based on the parameterization
F
if for every
\TSD
-object
X
,
\Recon(X)
is constructed as follows : consider the collection
\text{Pre}(X)
of all those parameter values
p\in\mathcal{P}
such that the data from
F(p)
transforms into
X
. Then
\Recon(X)
is built as the colimit of all dynamical systems arising from such
F(p)
. One analogously defines outer approximations based on the parameterization
F
.

The notions of exactness, consistency, simulation and inner / outer approximations are independent. Our first result in this section establishes how parameterization based inner or outer approximations also achieve a certain universality in terms of consistency or simulations :

Theorem 4

(Universality of inner / outer approximations)
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
be a parameterized subcategory of
DSMO
.

1.

Inner and outer approximations based on
F
exist.

2.

The inner approximation is universal in the following sense : Suppose
\Recon^{\prime}:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
is another reconstruction functor which is a simulator with respect to the parameterization, i.e., there is a natural transformation
\alpha:\Recon^{\prime}\circ\Data\circ F\Rightarrow\Forget\circ F
. Then there is an even more fundamental natural transformation
\beta:\Recon^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Recon
such that
\alpha
is related to
\beta
as
\alpha=\beta\circ\left(\Data\circ F\right)
.

3.

The outer approximation is universal in the following sense : Suppose
\Recon^{\prime}:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
is another reconstruction functor which is consistent with respect to the parameterization, i.e., there is a natural transformation
\alpha:\Forget\circ F\Rightarrow\Recon^{\prime}\circ\Data\circ F
. Then there is an even more fundamental natural transformation
\beta:\Recon\Rightarrow\Recon^{\prime}
such that
\alpha
is related to
\beta
as
\alpha=\left(\Data\circ F\right)\circ\beta
.

The notion of parameterized inner or outer approximations are right and left Kan extensions in the language of category theory :

\hbox to150.83pt{\vbox to40.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 75.41364pt\lower-21.11674pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-75.41364pt}{-16.36456pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -104.35 -22.64)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(23)

Theorem
4
is a direct restatement of the existence and universality of Kan extensions. See Section
11.3
for more details. Kan extensions are a consequence of purely diagrammatic calculus and have been used to interpret best-approximations in various other contexts. Some examples are topological data analysis
(
38
,
39
,
40
, e.g.)
, general data-science
(
41
,
40
, e.g.)
, and the theory of programming
(
42
,
43
,
44
, e.g.)
. Theorem
4
thus presents a direct advantage of the categorical path taken in this article. Since observation based inner / outer approximations are a special case of general parametrized inner / outer approximations we have the following immediate corollary to Theorem
4
by taking
\mathcal{P}=\textbf{\text{DSM}}
and
F=\iota_{obs}
as shown in (
23
) :

Corollary 5
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold.

1.

Observation based inner and outer approximations based on
F
exist.

2.

The inner approximation is universal in the following sense : Suppose
\Recon^{\prime}:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
is another reconstruction functor which is a simulator with respect to the parameterization, i.e., there is a natural transformation
\alpha:\Recon^{\prime}\circ\Data\circ F\Rightarrow\Forget\circ F
. Then there is an even more fundamental natural transformation
\beta:\Recon^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Recon
such that
\alpha
is related to
\beta
as
\alpha=\beta\circ\left(\Data\circ F\right)
.

3.

The outer approximation is universal in the following sense : Suppose
\Recon^{\prime}:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
is another reconstruction functor which is consistent with respect to the parameterization, i.e., there is a natural transformation
\alpha:\Forget\circ F\Rightarrow\Recon^{\prime}\circ\Data\circ F
. Then there is an even more fundamental natural transformation
\beta:\Recon\Rightarrow\Recon^{\prime}
such that
\alpha
is related to
\beta
as
\alpha=\left(\Data\circ F\right)\circ\beta
.

Equation (
21
) is a special case of (
22
). The role of the generic functor
F
in the upper-left corner of (
22
) is played by
\iota_{obs}
in (
21
). A reconstruction functor
\Recon
is defined on datasets in general, and is oblivious of the underlying source. If
\Recon
satisfies the functorial equality in (
21
), then
\Recon
achieves consistency on the restricted class of data-sources encoded by the functor
F
.

We have been fixing a parameterization
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
and looking for reconstructions which attain optimality in various sense. One can examine Diagram
19
from the opposite direction. One can fix an arbitrary reconstruction
\Recon
and then try to characterize the
DSMO
objects which are faithfully recreated by
\Recon
. One can make the following general statement :

Theorem 6
.

Given any reconstruction functor
\Recon:\TSD\to\textbf{\text{DS}}
, the collection of dynamical systems with measured orbits which allow a reconstruction form a subcategory of
DSMO
.

Theorem
6
assumes that the arrow labeled
\Recon
in Diagram (
22
) is given. The statement of the theorem is that given such a
\Recon
the arrows
F
and
G
exist. The latter pair of arrows forms an
equalizer
diagram, and the existence of an equalizer follows directly from the basic properties of
Cat
, the category of categories. An unspecified reconstruction functor
\Recon
makes it impossible to determine the collection of objects and semi-conjugacies in
DSMO
which are successfully recreated by
\Recon
. In spite of this lack of specificity, Theorem
6
infers a categorical structure purely by categorical arguments.
We next present our main result of this chapter :

Theorem 7
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then an observation-based inner approximation is also observation-exact.

Theorem
7
shows that even if the reconstruction strategy is simply set to be the simplest dynamics generating the given dataset, then it achieves consistency if the dataset comes from a full observation. Diagram (
1
) represents the most general situation of dynamics, data and reconstruction, and it is in general hard to equate reconstruction with the original source. Theorem
7
provides a sufficient and commonly occurring condition under which this is possible. Theorem
7
is proved in Section
11.10
. The main idea behind the proof is that inner and outer extensions correspond to categorical constructs called
right
and
left Kan extensions
, explained in detail in Appendix
11.3
. The following corollary investigates the case when the data available is a full shift :

Corollary 8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\textbf{\text{Topo}}
or
Meas
and
\mathcal{C}_{obs}=\mathrm{FinSet}
. If a time series data
X
is given by
X_{n}=\mathbb{A}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{A}
is a fixed set of alphabets, then we have

\mathrm{Lan}_{\Data}{\Forget}(X)=\left(\sigma,\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}\right).

This corollary follows from an argument similar to
6
. The only difference is that we consider
(\sigma,\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}},\pi_{0}:\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{A})
as the candidate object of reconstruction.

Example 19
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}=\textbf{\text{Meas}}
,
\Omega=[0,1]
and
\Phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be defined by

\Phi(x)=\begin{cases}2x&(0\leq x<\frac{1}{2})\\
2x-1&(\frac{1}{2}\leq x\leq 1).\end{cases}

We define an observable
\phi
by

\phi(x)=\begin{cases}0&(0\leq x<\frac{1}{2})\\
1&(\frac{1}{2}\leq x\leq 1).\end{cases}

Obviously,
\Data(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)_{n}=\{0,1\}^{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By Corollary
8
, the result of the reconstruction based on this data is the binary full shift:

\mathrm{Lan}_{\Data}{\Forget}\left(\Data(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)\right)=\left(\sigma,\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\right).

This reconstruction is exact.

Our category theoretic investigation of the entire process of dynamics to data generation, followed by a reconstruction back to a dynamical system allows us to make some statements of great generality such as in Theorems
4
and
6
. However, a categorical treatment tends to lose sight of implementation details and convergence properties of specific reconstruction strategies. Our approach makes no assumptions on the domain or dynamics, beyond the minimal and broad assumptions on the domain type in Assumption
1
. Thus, the usual tools of dynamical systems analysis, such as assumptions on positive Lyapunov exponents, rotational nature, Smale horseshoe structures are unavailable. This lack of specific details is not a disadvantage but an essential feature of any mathematical characterization of data-driven techniques. The commonly used data-driven approaches, such as Koopman based
[
45
,
4
,
3
]
, Ulam’s method
(
46
,
47
, e.g.)
, interpolation based
[
48
]
and symbolic dynamics based
(
49
,
50
, e.g.)
, avoid any assumption on the dynamics and instead utilize collective properties such as dense subsets having desirable topological or measure-theoretic properties.

Definition 24

(Ceiling and floor structure)
.

A category
\mathcal{P}
is said to be equipped with a
floor structure
if there is a functor
\lambda:\Lambda\to\mathcal{P}
satisfying :

1.

There is a functor
\lambda^{*}:\mathcal{P}\to\Lambda
and a natural transformation
\Id_{\mathcal{P}}\Rightarrow\lambda\circ\lambda^{*}
.

2.

Every left slice in
\Lambda
is finite.

A functor
\lambda:\Lambda\to\mathcal{P}
is said to have
ceiling structure
if condition (i) above reads
\lambda\circ\lambda^{*}\Rightarrow\Id_{\mathcal{P}}
, and in (ii) above, right (instead of left) slices are finite.

This definition is inspired by the familiar ”ceiling” and ”floor” operations in arithmetic. The are functorial and obey the above relations with the inclusion of the integers in the reals, although
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
does not have finite right slices.

Theorem 9

(Conditions for computability)
.

Consider a parameterization
F:\mathcal{P}\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
, and suppose that
\mathcal{P}
has a floor / ceiling structure. Then the
F
based inner / outer approximations are computable.

Theorem
9
is a direct consequence Lemma
11.4
presented later in Section
11.3
. For every integer
L\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Lambda^{(L)}
be the subcategory
\Lambda
spanned by objects whose left-slice size is no larger than
L
. Then the category
\Lambda
is the direct-limit or colimit of the following sequence of inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

One similarly obtains a gradation for right-finite categories. The following flowchart presents a method for computing an inner approximation by utilizing this gradation :

Pick an object
X
from
\TSD

Left slice
\left[\Data\circ F\;;\;X\right]
in
\mathcal{P}

Left slice
\left[\Data\circ F\;;\;X\right]^{(L)}
in
\mathcal{P}^{(L)}

Image of the left slice
\left[\Data\circ F\;;\;\right]^{(L)}
under
\Forget

Compute the colimit
\left(\Omega_{L},\Phi_{L}\right)
of this finite diagram in
C

The diagram

\left[\Data\circ F\;;\;X\right]^{(L)}\to\textbf{\text{DS}}

is a colimit of the diagrams

\left[fa\;;\;x\right]^{(L)}\to\textbf{\text{DS}}

\colim_{L\to\infty}\left(\Omega_{L},\Phi_{L}\right)
equals
\left(\Omega,\Phi\right)
which is the inner approximation
\mathrm{Lan}_{\Data F\lambda}{\Forget\lambda}(X)
=
\mathrm{Lan}_{\Data F}{\Forget}(X)

The procedure for computing outer approximations is analogous. Two useful examples of the second parameterizing category
\Lambda
are
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
and
\Delta
, the simplex category. Theorem
9
provides a framework for computability in dynamical systems. Any choice of this triple
\left(\Lambda,\mathcal{P},\lambda\right)
leads to a distinct computation paradigm. Note that if
\Lambda
is a category with finite left slices and
\lambda:\Lambda\to\textbf{\text{DSMO}}
is an embedding, then the image subcategory
\mathcal{P}:=\ran\lambda
is endowed with a floor structure. Thus an embedding of any category with finite left-slices lends a floor structure to at least one subcategory of
DSMO
. Characterizing the maximal such subcategory is an open task. Even with the trivial choice
\mathcal{P}:=\ran\lambda
there are some interesting computational paradigms

Example 20

(Quasiperiodic approximation)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{S}
is a dense and countable collection of irrational numbers, with some ordering
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots
. For every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, let
\text{Rot}_{s}
denote the quasiperiodic rotation on
S^{1}
with rotation number
s
. Consider the subcategory
\mathcal{P}
of
DS
spanned by all Cartesian products of finitely many such rotations. Each dynamical system in this collection is a quasiperiodic torus rotation. The natural preorder among the finite subsets of
\mathcal{S}
lead to a pre-ordered structure
\Lambda
among
\mathcal{P}
. Thus
\mathcal{P}
has a floor structure. An inner approximation with respect to
\mathcal{P}
leads to a quasiperiodic approximation of the data. This is the basis of all techniques such as DMD
(
51
, e.g.)
and spectral approximation of the Koopman operator
[
3
,
52
]
.

Example 21

(Approximation by subshifts)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
S_{n}
be the full shift on
n
symbols. For each
m<n
,
S_{m}
may be embedded within
S_{n}
in
{}^{n}C_{m}
-many ways. Next note that for any pair of embeddings as shown below

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

one has a pushout diagram resulting in a new sub-shift which is the gluing of
S_{n_{1}}
and
S_{n_{2}}
along their embedded images of
S_{m}
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the collection of all sub-shifts obtained by finitely many such gluings. Such a
\mathcal{P}
has a floor structure and the inner-approximation it provides attempts to model time series data as chaotic signals of various combinatorial complexity.

Finally, we comment on the impossibility of general and exact reconstruction scheme. In practice, only countable number of spaces are available as observable objects and therefore amenable to numerical procedures while there are uncountably many isomorphism classes of dynamical systems. Thus, as long as the reconstruction scheme only yields observable dynamics, there should be dynamical systems that cannot be reconstructed. More generally, we conjecture the following result that will exclude possibility of a universal data-driven reconstruction technique that provides an exact reconstruction.

Conjecture 1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
has uncountably many isomorphism classes of objects, while
\mathcal{C}_{obs}
has countably many. Then, for any reconstruction scheme
\Recon
, the subcategory of exact reconstructions (Theorem
6
) is a proper sub-category. In other words, there are DSMO objects not reconstructible by
\Recon
.

Further search for inner / outer approximation paradigms is an interesting avenue for investigation.

Conclusions

The major realizations we have reached are :

1.

The process of data generation from dynamical systems being measured along orbits is functorial.

2.

Time series data can be modeled as a chain of finite sequences of data, with decreasing information content.

3.

The limiting behavior of consistent reconstruction algorithms are functorial.

4.

Inner and outer approximations exist, additional conditions need to be imposed to ensure that they are computable or exact.

Category theory makes up for the lack of attention to individual detail by offering a collective perspective. A classical example is homology, which is a record of all the possible ways chain complexes map into a topological space. The countable collection of simplices and simplicial morphisms provide a computational as well as descriptive framework within the collection of uncountably infinitely many topological objects. Our analysis, along with Theorems
4
–
9
points towards an a similar possibility in the field of dynamical systems and data-driven reconstruction.

11  Appendix

11.1  The notion of colimits

Suppose
J
and
\mathcal{X}
are two categories. Then for any object
x\in ob(\mathcal{X})
there is a constant functor that sends every object of
J
into
x
, and every morphism into
\Id_{x}
. This functor is denoted as
x:J\to\mathcal{X}
. Given a functor
F:J\to\mathcal{X}
, a
co-cone
for
F
is a natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow x
from
F
to a constant functor
x
. The object
x
is called the
tip
of the co-cone. A
colimit
of
F
is a universal co-cone, that means it is a co-cone
\bar{\eta}:F\Rightarrow\bar{x}
such that for any other co-cone
\eta:F\Rightarrow x
, there is a unique
\phi:\bar{x}\to x
such that
\eta=\phi\circ\bar{\eta}
. The tip
\bar{x}
of the universal co-cone is also called the colimit of the functor
F
. Colimits are closely related to their dual notion :
limits
.

Colimits and limits are a sweeping generalization of various fundamental constructs in mathematics, such as limits, convergence, min, max, union, intersection, inverse images, and gluing. The functor
J
can interpreted as a pattern and
F
as a diagram existing within
\mathcal{X}
in the shape of
J
.
J
may be finite or infinite. For example a terminal object is thus the colimit of the identity functor
\Id_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
. The colimit
\bar{x}
provides a sense of completion or termination of the pattern. Colimits may not exist, but if they do, they are unique up to isomorphism.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is called
complete
(resp. cocomplete) if for any category
J
, any functor
F:J\to\mathcal{C}
has a limit (resp. colimit). In our case, if the context category
\Context
is either
\textbf{\text{Topo}},\textbf{\text{Meas}},\textbf{\text{Set}}
, it is co-complete.

Property 4
.

A category satisfying Assumption
1
is complete and cocomplete.

Property
4
will be crucial in our study of Kan extensions later.
Thus topological, measure-theoretic as well as set-theoretic dynamical systems have colimits. One of the simplest notions of limits is products. Categorical products are a generalization of the usual Cartesian products of sets, topological spaces or metric spaces. In all these contexts, if one has an indexed family of objects
\left\{A_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
, then one can form a product object
A:=\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
which lies in the same context as the
A_{i}
. We shall use the symbols
\prod
and
\times
interchangeably to denote a Categorical product. For each
i\in I
there is a
projection
morphism
\pi_{i}:A\to A_{i}
. In the familiar contexts mentioned above, the projections are the usual coordinate-wise projections. In a general category, they are defined by a
universal
property
(
53
, see)
. When the index set
I=\mathbb{N}
and all the
A_{i}
-s are the same object
\mathbb{A}
, the product is denoted as
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
. In the usual set theoretic interpretation
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
denotes the collection of all infinite sequences of elements of
\mathbb{A}
, indexed by
0,1,\ldots
. If
\mathbb{A}
is endowed with a topology, then
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}
inherits the product topology.

The following classical result guarantees the existence of (co)-limits under a simple condition :

Lemma 11.1
.

If a category
\mathcal{C}
has all
J
limits and / or colimits, then so does the functor category
\mathbb{F}\left(A\;;\;\mathcal{C}\right)
for any category
A
.

11.2  Adjunction

An adjunction between categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
is a pair of functors
F:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
,
G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that there is natural isomorphism
\Phi
between the following two Set-valued functors

\begin{split}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(F\cdot;\cdot)&=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\left(F\times\Id_{\mathcal{C}}\right):\mathcal{D}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\textbf{\text{Set}}.\\
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(\cdot;G\cdot)&=\Hom\circ\left(\Id_{\mathcal{D}}\times G\right):\mathcal{D}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\textbf{\text{Set}}.\end{split}

(24)

This means that for every
c\in ob(\mathcal{C}),d\in ob(\mathcal{D})
, there is a bijection

\Phi_{d,c}:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(Fd;c)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(d;Gc),

which is also natural in the following sense :

\forall\;\hbox to64.37pt{\vbox to47.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 32.18282pt\lower-23.5506pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(25)

Equivalently, adjunction means :

\forall h\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(Fd;c),\quad\Phi_{d^{\prime},c^{\prime}}\left(\psi\circ h\circ F(\phi)\right)=G(\psi)\circ\Phi_{d,c}h\circ\phi.

(26)

Note that in (
24
), the relation between
F
and
G
is not symmetric.
F
is called left adjoint to
G
,
G
is called the right adjoint to
F
. This relation is depicted as
F\dashv G
.

Adjunction is a generalization of the notion of inverse. Left or right inverses if they exist, are left and right adjoints. Thus a pair of invertible functors form an adjunction pair. An inversion is based on a one-to-one correspondence between objects in the domain and codomain categories. In the absence of such a correspondence, one can hope for the closest approximation to an inversion. The notion of ”closeness” or ”proximity” is borne by morphisms within a category. Morphisms are directional in nature. So an approximation can be from the left or from the right, leading to the notions of left and right adjoints respectively. Adjunction is the basis for the concept of Kan extensions.

11.3  Kan extensions

Kan extensions
(
23
,
54
,
55
, e.g.)
are universal constructions which generalize the notion of constrained optimization to the categorical context. Suppose we have the following arrangement of categories and functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

(27)

When we regard
K
to be an inclusion of
X
into
D
, Kan extensions can be interpreted as the most general generalizations of the functor
F
to the domain
D
. One can alternatively interpret
F
as the constraint of the optimization problem where we seek the best functor
D\to E
that satisfies it.

Definitions are as follows. A
left Kan extension
of
F
along
K
is a functor
\Lan_{K}{F}:D\to E
along with a minimum natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow\Lan_{K}{F}\circ K
. Moreover, this pair
\left(\Lan_{K}{F},\eta\right)
is minimum / universal in the sense that for every other functor
H:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow H\circ K
, there is a unique natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:\Lan_{K}{F}\Rightarrow H
s.t.
\gamma=\left(\tilde{\gamma}\star\Id_{K}\right)\circ\eta
. This is shown in the diagram below.

\mathcal{L}:=\Lan_{F}(K),\quad\hbox to179.99pt{\vbox to81.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.99356pt\lower-41.82285pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

The
right Kan extension
of
F
along
K
is a dual notion to the left Kan extension and the definition is similar except the directions of arrows. It is a functor
\Ran_{K}{F}:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\epsilon:\Ran_{K}{F}\circ K\Rightarrow F
, which is maximum among similar pairs of functor
H:D\to E
and natural transformation
\gamma:H\circ K\Rightarrow F
in the sense there exists a unique natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:H\Rightarrow\Ran_{K}F
such that
\gamma=\epsilon\circ\left(\tilde{\gamma}\star\Id_{K}\right)
.
If
E
is a co-complete category, the left Kan extension always exists. Similarly if
E
is a complete category, the right Kan extension always exists. In case both left and right Kan extensions of
F
along
K
exist, they combine to produce the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Lan}_{K}{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ran}_{K}{F}}

(28)

It is often possible to explicitly determine action of a Kan extension on objects, as shown below :

Lemma 11.2
.

Consider the arrangement of (
27
). Then

\begin{split}\mbox{If }E\mbox{ is cocomplete, }\mbox{ then }&\Lan_{K}{F}(d)=\colim\left\{Fx\;:\;Kx\to d\right\}\\
\mbox{If }E\mbox{ is complete, }\mbox{ then }&\Ran_{K}{F}(d)=\lim\left\{Fx\;:\;d\to Kx\right\}\\
\end{split}

(29)

This construction is known as the
pointwise
definition of Kan extensions. The colimit and limit in (
29
) are along slices of the object
d
along
K
. Note that each slice, left or right, can be interpreted as a constraint on the objects of
K
. The act of finding limits or colimits is analogous to finding the minimum or maximum under this constraint.

Lemma 11.3
.

Suppose Property
4
hold. Then any arrangement of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
DS
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

has a right and left Kan extensions of
G
along
F
.

Proof.

Since by Property
4

\Context
is (co)-complete, the dynamics category
\textbf{\text{DS}}=\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)
is (co)-complete too. Thus by Lemma
11.2
, the Kan extensions exist, and have the pointwise definition expressed in (
29
).
∎

Pre- and post- adjoints

Let
T:A\to B
be a functor. Then
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
pre-right adjoint
to
T
if
TT^{*}\Rightarrow Id_{B}
. Similarly,
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
post-right adjoint

(
20
,
56
, e.g.)
to
T
if
Id_{B}\Rightarrow TT^{*}
. The next lemma presents some useful conditions under which Kan extensions are preserved under pre-compositions.

Lemma 11.4
.

(
56
, Lem 7.5)
Consider the following arrangement of functors
a,b,f
as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{L/Ran}_{f}(b)}

(30)

1.

If
C
is co-complete and
a
has a post-right adjoint, then
\mathrm{Lan}_{b}{f}=\mathrm{Lan}_{ba}{fa}
.

2.

If
C
is complete and
a
has a pre-right adjoint, then
\mathrm{Ran}_{b}{f}=\mathrm{Ran}_{ba}{fa}
.

3.

In particular if
C
is co-complete and
a
has a right inverse then
\mathrm{Lan}_{b}{f}=\mathrm{Lan}_{ba)}{fa}
.

4.

In particular if
C
is complete and
a
has a right inverse then
\mathrm{Ran}_{b}{f}=\mathrm{Ran}_{ba}{fa}
.

11.4  Topological concrete categories

Topological concrete categories provide a powerful framework for abstracting and unifying concepts across different areas of mathematics and its applications. They are categories whose objects are sets equipped with some ”topological-like” structure, and whose morphisms are functions that preserve that structure. A category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be topological concrete over a category
\mathcal{S}
if there is a functor
U:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
with the following property : given any object
X\in\mathcal{S}
, any indexed family
\left\{\Omega_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{S}
-morphisms
f_{i}:X\to U(\Omega_{i})
, then :

1.

there exists a
\mathcal{C}
-object
\bar{\Omega}
such that
U(\bar{\Omega})=X
;

2.

there exist
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms
g_{i}:\bar{\Omega}\to\Omega_{i}
such that for each
i\in I
,
U(g_{i})=f_{i}
.

This arrangement
\left\{g_{i}:\bar{\Omega}\to\Omega_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
is universal in the sense that for any other arrangement
\left\{g^{\prime}_{i}:\bar{\Omega^{\prime}}\to\Omega_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
and a morphism
\phi:U(\bar{\Omega^{\prime}})\to X
satisfying
U(g^{\prime}_{i})=\phi\circ f_{i}
for each
i\in I
, there is a unique morphism
\psi:\bar{\Omega^{\prime}}\to\bar{\Omega}
such that

1.

U(\psi)=\phi
;

2.

g^{\prime}_{i}=\psi\circ g_{i}
for each
i\in I
.

The object
\bar{\Omega}
is called the
initial lift
of the diagram
\left\{g_{i}:\bar{\Omega}\to\Omega_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
. It is the simplest or minimal object in
\Context
which allows that diagram in
\mathcal{S}
to be realized. In our article, the role of
\mathcal{S}
is played by
Set
.

Many fundamental categories in mathematics, such as the category
Topo
of topological spaces or
Meas
of measurable spaces, are examples of topological concrete categories. This framework allows theorems proven in the context of topological concrete categories to be immediately applied to these diverse areas.

A key property of topological concrete categories is that the forgetful functor
U
allows certain fundamental constructions in topology, like forming products, subspaces (equalizers), and quotient spaces (coequalizers) (which are specific types of limits and colimits), can be defined by performing the set-theoretic operation and then equipping the resulting set with the appropriate initial or final topology/structure. A few of the important properties of topological concrete categories are :

Property 5
.

If Assumption
1
holds, then there is a functor
U^{*}:\textbf{\text{Set}}\to\Context
which is a left adjoint of
U
.

Property 6
.

If Assumption
1
holds, then the functor
U
is faithful, i.e., injective on morphisms.

Property 7
.

If Property
5
holds, then the functor
U
maps
1_{\Context}
into a single element set in
Set
.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a topological concrete category and
U:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Set}
be the associated forgetful functor. Then

1.

\mathcal{C}
is complete and cocomplete.

2.

A morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is monic (epic) if
Uf:UX\to UY
is injective (surjective).

3.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
f:Y\to Z
,
p:X\to Y^{\prime}
and
q:Y^{\prime}\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. If
Ug\circ Uf=Uq\circ Up
, then
q\circ p=g\circ f
.

4.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. Then, we can define an epi-mono factorization of
f
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{im}\,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

by taking
\mathrm{im}\,f
to be the final lift of the sink
UX\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{im}\,Uf
in
\mathrm{Set}
.

11.5  Functors induced between comma categories

One can derive a functor between these two comma categories, as explained in the following lemma.

Lemma 11.6
.

(
57
, Prop 6)
Consider the arrangement of categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}

(31)

Then there is an induced functor between comma categories

\Psi:\left[F\;;\;G\right]\to\left[F^{\prime}\;;\;G^{\prime}\right],

(32)

Moreover, the following commutation holds with the marginal functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F\;;\;G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F^{\prime}\;;\;G^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{2}}

(33)

Note that the two horizontal rows of (
31
) correspond to two comma categories. Lemma
11.6
states that if comma categories are intertwined in the manner of (
31
), then there is a functor between these comma categories which contain the vertical functors as projections.

11.6  Categorical view of subshifts

Proof of Lemma
6.1

The result follows from the diagram in
\Context
shown below on the left.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{i}S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{i}S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{0}(S^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(S^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{i}U(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{i}U(S^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(S^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{n}(U(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{0}(U(S^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Uf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Uf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(f_{0})}

The diagram on the right is the image of the one on the left under the functor
U
. The two sides of the identity in Lemma
6.1
correspond to the two paths from the top-left to the bottom-right corner of the diagram. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.1
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2

The pullback square in the construction of cylinders is the categorical analog of inverses. One of the properties of pullback squares is that if any morphism in the original vee-shaped diagram is an injection, then so is its parallel morphism in the pullback square. The morphism opposite to the morphism from
\Cylinder(w)\to S
originates from the terminal element, and is injective by default. This makes the morphism
\Cylinder(w)\to S
injective too. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.2
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3

We set up the map
R_{m}:U\word_{m+n}(S)\to U\word_{m}(S^{\prime})
by

R_{m}(\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{m+n}):=((Uf_{0})(\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})(Uf_{0})(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\cdots\xi_{n+1})\cdots(Uf_{0})(\xi_{m}\xi_{m+1}\cdots\xi_{m+n})).

This is well-defined by Lemma
6.1
. By applying Lemma
5.1
, we can verify the existence of the desired morphism
f^{(m)}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.3
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
6.4

The proof follows directly from the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{m+n}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{m}(S^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{0}(S^{\prime\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(m)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{0}}

This completes the proof of Lemma
6.4
. ∎

where commutativity of inner triangles and rectangles have already been established. The identity to be proved lies on the periphery of this diagram. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.7
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
2

We only check for functoriality for (
12
) as the proof for (
13
) will be analogous. Fix a
DSM
object
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)
. Consider the epi-mono factorization of the morphism
\xi_{(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)}
defined above :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota Y)^{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)}}

Then the correspondence of
(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)
with
\Obs(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)
is functorial. Its action on any morphism
(h,A):(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)\to(\Omega^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
is the unique morphism shown below as a dotted, green line that enables the commutation :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs(\Omega,\Phi,\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs(\Omega^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota Y)^{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota Y^{\prime})^{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Obs(h,A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{*}}

The test for compositionality is routine and will be omitted. This completes the proof of Theorem
2
. ∎

11.8  Time series data

Compositionality in
\TSD

Suppose there is another morphism
(l,\psi):Y\to Z
. Then
(l,\psi)
composes with
(k,\phi)
as shown below, yielding a morphism
(k+l,\psi\circ\phi):X\to Z
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

We can check that the composition is associative by using the property
(\psi\circ\phi)_{n}=\psi_{n}\circ\phi_{n+l}
. ∎

Lemma 11.8
.

Let
\Context
be as in Assumption
1
and
\mathbb{A}
be an object in
\Context
. For each
n
, we have

\mathfrak{f}_{n}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n}=\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n-1},\quad\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n-1}\circ\sigma=\eta_{n}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n}.

Proof.

The result follows from universality of the projection
\pi_{\mathbb{N}\to n-1}
along with the diagrams which hold for every
1\leq i\leq n-1
:

The following technical lemma is needed for the proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 11.9
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds,
I
is an indexing set,
c
is an object,
\left\{a_{i}\;:\;i\in I\right\}
is a family of objects in
\Context
and
f_{i}:c\to a_{i}
is a family of morphisms. Next suppose that
a
is a subobject of the product
\prod_{i\in I}a_{i}
. Then if there is a morphism
\phi
which creates the commutation shown below

Proof of Theorem
3

We check the functoriality of the construction in (
17
). Let
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
be an
n
-morphism. We set up
\phi_{m}:\word_{m+n}(S)\to\word_{m}(S)
using Lemma
6.3
. By a direct calculation in
\mathrm{Set}
, we can check that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\word_{m+n}(S)}

Thus
